## Reading guide

Here is how to read this page. Each card is one thing this author teaches, explained for a beginner first and then in full. The label above a group of cards tells you what kind of thing they are: a principle is a rule the author lives by, a concept is a way of seeing things, a technique is how to do something, a drill is practice you repeat, an exercise is a task you do once or on a schedule, a list is a set the author gives, a script is words to say, an example is a story that makes a point, a glossary term is a word the author defines, a procedure is steps in order, a claim is a statement of fact, a mistake is what to avoid, and a dataset is numbers to refer to.

Cards are grouped by Domain, then by Stage, then by type. Cards without a stage sit under Unassigned. Every card ends with its sources, as a resource id and a page or timestamp. Each quote behind a source was checked against the source text, so you can trust it and go look it up.

Three sections at the end help you keep the picture current. The Evolution timeline shows where the author changed their teaching over time, oldest first, with what changed. Contradictions and open questions shows where a newer source conflicts with an older one, plus cards flagged for review. The Obsolescence watch shows cards whose newest source is older than 7 years or that were superseded, so you know what may no longer be the author's view.

## Cards: Money

### Money / Property Investor / Social Housing

**Principles**

#### A leader cannot afford negativity

Also known as: You're only as good as your leader, Your team follows the leader

People ask Shaun how he stays so positive. His answer: he cannot afford not to. Negative thinking grows, spreads onto your team and lowers their performance, so as a leader your own energy is a business asset you have to protect.

- If Shaun starts thinking negatively, negative energy comes into his life and grows day by day.
- Taken a level deeper: a negative leader pushes negativity onto the team, which affects their performance, and soon the whole organisation is full of negativity.
- A team is only as good as its leader. Leaders must make sure their own energy is correct.
- That means working on yourself and filling your own cup so you can keep pouring for others.

Sources:
- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (10:00)
- R-YT-O1krmmSq5Zo (01:02)

#### A positive community does not tolerate negativity

The academy community works because everyone moves in the same direction and helps each other up. When someone brings negativity in, the hosts tell them straight that it will not be tolerated. Protecting the group's energy is part of leading it.

- Keep everyone moving the same direction: growing, pushing to the next level, closing deals, helping each other.
- When negativity appears, address it directly: 'this will not be tolerated, we are a positive community, we lift each other up'.

- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (40:02)
- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (41:02)

#### A professional brand and image close deals

Also known as: A professional image is make or break

People judge you on how you look, how you speak and what they find when they search you online. Providers and landlords buy from people they know, like and trust. A clean, professional presence makes relationship building easier and deals faster.

- People will judge you on the way you look, on your brand, on how you present yourself and how you speak
- People will search you online, so you need an online presence: social media, a business page, a personal brand or a website
- You do not strictly need a website, but it looks much better
- People buy from people they know, like and trust
- A professional image and a great brand help you close more deals quickly

- R-YT-aRcmGZIV7Y4 (12:01)
- R-YT-E9HYGweF8Aw (20:01)

#### Adapt to every person; communication is skill number one

Also known as: Communication skills

Asked what he had to change about himself, the student says he had to accept that everybody is different and learn to deal with landlords and tenants on their own level. Shaun adds that communication is the number one skill you can learn, and you cannot do this role without it.

- Realise everybody is different: landlords and tenants sit on different levels and you must deal with all of them.
- Be able to adapt, chat, and deal with every issue you get.
- Communication is the number one skill you can learn.
- You cannot do this job without it.

#### An empty property is a liability, not an asset

Ambition and good properties count for nothing if you cannot fill them. Shaun's rule: build the referral pipeline before you build the portfolio, because an unfilled property costs you money every month instead of earning it.

- Applies most to supported accommodation, where referrals do not arrive on their own.
- Fix: build relationships with councils and homeless shelters first, so there is an influx of referrals before you take on properties.

#### Anyone can have a lucky year; lasting takes habits

Also known as: Bulletproof mentality, Building is one thing, lasting is another

Shaun's closing point: building something is easy compared with keeping it. Anyone can have one or two lucky years. To build over decades, and leave something that outlives you, you need habits, the strength to stand firm and a bulletproof mentality.

- Building is one thing; anyone can build, and anyone can have a lucky year or two.
- Building in decades, something that lasts a very long time and may outlive you, is different.
- For that you have to build habits, stand strong and have a bulletproof mentality.
- The seven habits are the ones Shaun credits for getting him there.

#### Attitude and willingness to learn beat experience

Shaun had no experience in social housing when he became a provider. What carried him was the attitude that it had to work and a willingness to learn. If you are waiting to feel qualified, start anyway and figure it out as you go.

- He became a provider with no experience and no idea what he was doing.
- What he had instead: the attitude, the willingness to learn, and the conviction that this has to work.
- He figured it out and found his first property in Birmingham about two weeks after becoming a provider.

#### Be authentic and document your journey

Also known as: Be authentic with whatever you do, Same energy online and in person, Keep that same energy, Authenticity, Build an authentic personal brand, Document your journey, Share your journey

Status: needs review

Shaun's biggest tip for anyone going online with their property journey is to be authentic. Be the same person on camera as you are in the room, and do not pose as an expert: saying 'this is what I'm up to' is enough. Authenticity is what makes you different. Without it you are like everyone else, and there is no reason to watch or follow you.

- Document what happens, including the hard parts, and share information as much as you can.
- Share your journey in the most authentic way possible; authenticity is the brand for Shaun, Luigi and their academy.
- Be aware that sharing invites offence and problems in this generation; do it anyway.
- Beginner version: you do not need to come across as an expert. Say 'right guys, this is what I'm up to, this is what I'm doing'. Luigi started that way and it worked.
- The rule in practice: how the hosts are on social media and on YouTube is exactly how they are in person. No difference, the same energy every time, and they treat this as something that will not change as the channel grows.
- Why it works: sharing the truth is what makes people engage. The biggest compliment the hosts get is how authentic they come across.
- The flip side: if you are not authentic you are not yourself, so you are like everyone else. Then nothing makes people want to watch you or engage with you.

#### Be exceptional, not good

Councils judge you on inspections, maintenance and clear communication; these decide whether you last. Good is not enough, and the author has watched many providers fall away. Put policies, procedures and systems in place from day one.

- Inspections, maintenance and clear communication mean everything to councils; they give you longevity
- Being good is not good enough; you need to be exceptional
- Have clear systems and processes to deliver an exceptional service
- Have all your policies and procedures in place to run the business effectively and efficiently
- Start as you mean to go on from day one
- Anything less than exceptional maintenance and compliance is not acceptable

#### Be in a recession-proof vehicle

Pick a business model that pays you whatever the economy does. After COVID the author's test for any strategy became: is it recession proof, free of peaks and troughs, and stable regardless of what happens? Temporary accommodation passed.

- What he wanted: recession proof, not full of peaks and troughs, safe regardless of what happens with the economy, the utmost financial stability.
- Serviced accommodation failed this test in COVID.
- Temporary accommodation passes it because the rate is set by the council and backed by the government, so income does not stop in a recession.

- When you speak to the council, be super clear about exactly what you can do.
- State your intentions plainly.

#### Become a professional problem solver

Also known as: The ship must continue to sail

The students who do best are not the ones with the easiest deals. They are the ones who expect problems, embrace them and treat solving them as part of the job. Kicked-in doors, slow councils and bumps in the road are normal; the business must keep sailing regardless.

- Go in expecting challenges, not an easy ride.
- Embrace the challenges and become a professional problem solver.
- Mindset line: there could be bumps in the road, but we will overcome them regardless, and the ship must continue to sail.
- Sandu is the example: not used to day-to-day problems at first, now a professional problem solver with nine deals.

#### Begin with the end in mind

Also known as: The 1,000 rooms target, Big goals to strive towards

Status: needs review

Shaun always runs towards a fixed number, currently 1,000 rooms, and decides what comes next only when he gets there. Without an end in sight you run and run, burn out and lose momentum, because you no longer know what you are working towards. Set the big goal first, then plan the next one from there.

- He has always had a number in mind: 1,000 rooms. When he reaches it he will make an informed decision about what he wants to do next, including any exit.
- Why it matters: if you just keep running with no end, you can burn out and lose the momentum you have built, because there is nothing you are working towards.
- His method: always have big goals you are striving towards. Once you hit one, ask what the next plan is.
- The interviewer expects him to hit 1,000 within the next couple of months rather than years.

#### Big success means big sacrifice

Also known as: Something has to give, A few years of tunnel vision for a lifetime of freedom

Shaun does not pretend work-life balance is easy. If you want great success, something has to give, and for him it has been relationships. His bet is to work with tunnel vision for a few years while he is young and has the energy, so he can have a lifetime of freedom later.

#### Build a profitable business before you teach it

Also known as: Learn first, build second, teach last

Shaun did not start by teaching. He learned, built a viable and profitable business, became credible in his niche, and only then built a community and started mentoring. The order matters: credibility comes from results, not from a course.

The order Shaun followed:
1. Learn and develop yourself first.
2. Build a viable, profitable business.
3. Reach the stage where you have enough experience to be credible in your industry and niche. For Shaun that niche is property and social housing only.
4. Build a community.
5. Start teaching and mentoring people.

#### Build a team to scale

You cannot run 35 properties alone. Shaun calls building a good team around you crucial for scaling. The student already has five people, a mix of full-time staff and helpers doing odd jobs, and is still hiring to cope with the workload.

- Building a good team around you is crucial to scale a business.
- It is not always the easiest thing to do.

#### Build active income before passive income

Also known as: Put the work in first, Do the groundwork before passive income

Everyone wants passive income and the freedom that comes with it. Shaun says people go wrong by not being willing to put the work in first. Have the goal of passive income, but build an active income first, and keep working even after the freedom arrives.

- The goal of time and location freedom is fine; Shaun says definitely have it.
- The mistake: wanting the freedom without being willing to do the work first.
- The order: build an active income first, put the work in, then aim for passive income.
- Shaun still puts the work in now that he has time and location freedom.
- It takes resilience to keep going through this stage.

#### Build cash flow first

Also known as: Cash flow before deposits

Shaun's starting rule for 2026: if he had to begin again with nothing, he would build monthly cash flow before saving for a buy-to-let deposit. Owning property stays the end goal, but cash flow gets you out of your job and funds the journey. You do not wait years for your first purchase.

- Do not start by trying to save huge deposits for buy-to-lets, and do not spend years waiting to buy your first property.
- Do not sit there saying you need money before you get started.
- Owning the asset is still the end goal, because it is always good to own the asset.
- Use quick cash flow strategies such as rent-to-rent and rent-to-social housing to build income fast.
- Run them alongside your property development journey, not instead of it.

#### Business is cutthroat: no excuses, get the result

Also known as: No second chances in business

Car sales taught Shaun that one bad month or one bad valuation gets you sacked on the spot. He says business is the same: there are no second chances. Do the work, drop the excuses and the victim mentality, and get the result.

- In car sales you always felt one step away from being sacked: one bad move, one bad part exchange valuation, one missed detail.
- People who did well for months were let go after a single bad month.
- Shaun's transfer to business: business is cutthroat and there are no second chances.
- The rules: do the work, do not give excuses, do not have a victim mentality, just get the result.

#### Business is peaks and troughs: prepare for the trough

Successful people and his father all warned Shaun: business, like life, is not smooth sailing. Markets change, people get ill, things go wrong even when today is good. He thought he was prepared; COVID three weeks after quitting showed him how soon it can happen.

- The warning he kept hearing from books and from people like his father: business is good now, but like life it brings personal issues, illness and market changes.
- Business is full of peaks and troughs; things going well today does not stop the market changing.
- There will be a time when things go wrong. Plan for it.
- Shaun's admission: he believed he was prepared but did not think it would happen so soon.

#### Cash flow is the heart of the business

Keep cash buffers. Cash flow is what keeps a business alive, and the speed at which money comes back to you decides how fast you can take the next deal. Being undercapitalised is one of the two reasons the author says people fail.

- Hold cash buffers
- Cash flow is the heart of any business
- Velocity of money matters: upfront incentives and deposits let you go again and again
- Being undercapitalised is a main cause of failure

Also known as: Kids forget what you say, not what you did, Actions speak louder than words

Shaun tells parents the best thing they can do for their children is to go after their own dreams in front of them. Kids forget what you say but never forget what you did. Seeing you fail and get back up teaches them the character they will need.

#### Choose carefully who goes into a big house

Also known as: Tenant mix in large HMOs

The more people share a house, the more care you need over who you move in. One wrong person in a house of many can spread trouble like wildfire, and two people who trigger each other can make the whole house a nightmare. Put extra energy and focus into placement for your bigger houses.

- Bigger houses need more energy and focus on who you are moving in
- Mistake: putting the wrong person into an environment with many people. Consequence: it spreads like wildfire onto everybody else.
- Mistake: putting two people together who trigger each other. Consequence: an absolute nightmare.
- Fix: treat placement as the crucial decision for a large house. Shaun's 14-person house was working well because of the people chosen for it, and he credits the team leader for that.

#### Clean and fit for purpose, not glamorous

The finish does not need to impress anyone. It needs to be clean, working and suited to the use. The author shows a plain kitchen with new worktops, cupboards, cooker and washing machine and calls it exactly right for this type of let.

- Aim for a clean, fit-for-purpose product, not a glamorous finish
- In this property the landlord replaced the worktops, the cupboards, the cooker and the washing machine

Sources:
- R-YT-4lFF_IDs08U (01:00)

#### Come from a place of value, not a sales pitch

Also known as: Lead with value

When you approach a shop or any potential customer, offer something useful first instead of asking them to buy. Shaun's point is that a cold ask gets "who are you?", while a free taster and a clear offer gets a conversation. Give first, then let them come back to you.

- Wrong approach: walk in and ask "you want my cakes? you want to sell my cakes?" The answer is "No, who are you?"
- Right approach: introduce yourself and what you make, offer free tasters, show your feedback, and ask for the opportunity
- Let them try it and get back to you, rather than pushing for a decision on the spot

Sources:
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (06:00)

#### Confuse them and you lose them

Also known as: Keep it simple, If you confuse them, you lose them, Explain it in simple terms

Agents and landlords do not know your jargon. If your pitch needs explaining, they switch off and say no. Shaun keeps it to one plain line, "we work with the council", and gets straight to the point.

- People do not understand "temporary accommodation" or "rent to rent". Explaining it confuses them, and confused people say no.
- Say instead: "We work with the council. We're looking for three and four bedroom properties." Direct, straight to the point.
- It is all about the way you come across; the same town that "doesn't work" for others worked within an afternoon.
- The full rapport recipe: build rapport, find common ground, keep it simple.

#### Councils are just people

Treat council staff as people, not as an institution. Build relationships with them on purpose and keep them up. That is how you get and keep contracts.

- Councils are just people; treat them like people
- Build relationships and be intentional about them
- Maintain the relationships over time
- Council staff are very busy, so do not waste their time

Sources:
- R-YT-E9HYGweF8Aw (02:01)
- R-YT-E9HYGweF8Aw (22:01)

#### Councils change, the tenant never does

Also known as: The one thing that never changes is the tenant

Councils cut budgets, restructure and change leadership, so any one scheme can end. What stays constant is the people who need housing. When a scheme closes, the council still has to put those tenants somewhere, so it creates a new scheme. Follow the tenants, not the scheme.

- Councils make changes, make cuts and bring in new leadership structures.
- The tenant never changes. The people are still there and the council has to put them somewhere.
- So councils create different schemes, put different processes in place and open different opportunities for landlords.
- Practical use: when a council tells you a scheme has ended, ask where the tenants are going and get involved in the replacement scheme.

Notes: Shaun gives this as one more tip specifically for temporary accommodation.

Sources:
- R-YT-PKg8fPvSkfw (1:34:00)

- The question to ask of any strategy: what vehicle gives me financial stability?

#### Find the provider first, not the property

Also known as: Provider first, Finding the property before the provider, Provider first, property second

Do not go looking for a property until you have a provider who wants to scale. Only the provider can tell you which areas, which property types and which rents make a deal work. Get that brief first, then go and find exactly what they asked for.

The order Sean insists on:
1. Find an active social housing provider that is looking to scale (start with the council, then Contracts Finder).
2. Ask them the three core questions: what areas, what specific property types, what they pay per property.
3. Only then start building relationships with agents and looking for the exact properties the provider needs.
4. Marry the provider's need and the property together; the deal follows.

The mistake he sees most often: people find the property first, before closing the council or a provider. They end up holding a property with no active provider that wants it, and they cannot judge whether the deal stacks because they never learned what the provider wanted.

#### Get in front of people; never close by email or phone

Also known as: Face to face closes, Building rapport, Get in front of people to build rapport, Be proactive and get in front of people, People that are proactive win the game, Get in front of people, Do not hide behind a laptop, Get from behind the laptop, Go face-to-face; velocity is crucial, Build and maintain relationships, Go directly to the source, Sending emails from behind the phone, Looking for the easy route

Phone and email are for arranging things and gathering information. The close happens face to face, or on Zoom if you cannot meet. People are busier and blunter on the phone; in front of you they have to give you their attention, and once rapport is built the close has far less friction. A landlord who says no on the phone can say yes in the room.

- On the phone people are busy, cut you off and can be a lot meaner. In person they are friendlier and have no choice but to give you their undivided attention.
- Email is great for finding out more information. Never try to close in the email.
- Order of play: email or phone to arrange, then meet face to face if possible, or a Zoom call if not; build the rapport; then go for the close. With rapport built there is less friction and the close is more seamless.
- A line to move a phone call to a meeting: "When are you in the office?"
- Get from behind the computer and the phone. Physically go and view properties, meet people and build rapport rather than working only from a screen.
- Example: Dave the landlord started very cold and warmed right up during the viewing. On the phone he would have said no. What worked was the basics: having a conversation, building rapport, finding common ground.

#### Keep both leases congruent

Also known as: Matching break clauses, The three-way partnership

The one condition that makes back-to-back leasing risk-free. Your lease with the landlord must mirror your lease with the provider: same terms, same break clauses. Then if the provider cancels, you can exit the landlord lease too, instead of being stuck paying for an empty property.

- The structure is a three-way partnership: landlord to you, you to the provider.
- You sign a commercial lease agreement on both sides.
- Whatever you signed with the provider, the contract with the landlord must be congruent: exactly the same terms and the same break clauses.
- Why: if the provider deal falls through, you are not lumbered with a property that becomes bad debt. If they cancel, you can get out of your contract too.
- As long as the contracts align, Shaun says the strategy literally becomes risk-free.

#### Keep the main thing the main thing

Also known as: Never give up on what's working, Keep watering the grass

When new opportunities keep landing on your desk, the safest move is to double down on the thing that already works. Shaun compares it to watering grass: keep watering and it keeps growing. You can start something new, but never let it take over from the business that is already paying you.

- Always do what is doing well. If something works, do not give it up; keep doubling down on it.
- Shaun's own case: social housing has worked extremely well since 2020, he has 500 rooms and says he has only scratched the surface, so looking into a new shiny penny is pointless for him.
- Starting something new is not wrong in itself. The rule is to keep the main thing the main thing.
- The mistake to avoid: something is working well, you make a new project the main thing, and you lose the original thing that was good in the first place.
- Reason: whatever you focus on most is what grows. Spread your focus and the original business stops growing.

- The VA arranged appointments and got tasks boxed off.
- Only Shaun did the sales and spoke to landlords, the work that really moved the needle.
- Scaling tip number one is leverage, and step one of that is getting yourself a VA.

#### Look at supply and demand in any business

Before Shaun chose property he asked the same question he asks of any business: is there more demand than supply? Property had a long track record of growth and, in the UK, a housing shortage he had heard about his whole life. That gap is what made it worth pursuing.

- Always look at any business through supply and demand
- Why property passed the test for him:
  - People he heard growing up called property a good investment, so ignoring it would have been ignorant
  - There is lots of historical data showing how property grows
  - The UK has had a housing crisis for as long as he has been alive and needs more properties
  - Many people are on universal credit and councils cannot house their tenants fast enough

#### Make fast decisions and commit

Also known as: The art of making fast decisions, Velocity

Entrepreneurs have to decide quickly and then commit. Overthinking turns into procrastination and no decision at all. Fast decisions also make people want to work with you, and they keep you level with competitors who live and breathe their business.

- Decision-making is crucial as an entrepreneur. Make the decision and commit to it, quickly.
- Overthinking leads to procrastinating and never deciding. Use the best case, worst case, likely case analysis to cut through it.
- People love to work with people who can make fast decisions. Velocity matters.
- The people you compete with are on it, living and breathing the business, absolutely obsessed. If you take ages to think about it and do not decide quickly, you will always fall short.

#### Manage your emotions

Also known as: Move logically, not emotionally, Deal with business logically, not emotionally, Logical point of view, not emotional

Shaun calls this one of the best pieces of advice he can give. If your emotions swing all over the place you will sabotage the opportunities in front of you, and as a leader your moods spread to your team. He watches himself closely and tries to act logically instead of emotionally.

- Learn to manage your emotions effectively or you will sabotage your own success. With your emotions all over the place you cannot condition your mind to spot opportunities, and you sabotage the ones already in front of you.
- Team effect: a negative, reactive, emotional leader whose mood drops at every little challenge passes that ethos to the team. They adopt the same mentality, and the leader becomes unapproachable.
- His practice: be very conscious of yourself and try to move very logically rather than emotionally.

A joint venture where you split the risk and the profit lets you scale far faster than sourcing alone. - Partner with one estate agent and they can give you lots of different deals. One good relationship can change your life in this business.

#### Partner with high-turnover providers

Also known as: Temporary and emergency accommodation first

Back-to-back leasing works with any provider, but choose the ones that need many properties fast. Temporary and emergency accommodation providers have high turnover and constant demand, so one relationship can scale. A children's home may give you one property and take a year to get ready.

- Temporary and emergency accommodation: high-level demand, high turnover, they usually need a lot of properties, and it is fast. This is where scaling makes sense.
- Children's home or care home: you might get one property, and it takes ages to get ready, possibly a year. All the work of finding the provider and the property, with no end goal beyond that one deal.
- Rule: pick providers that are willing to scale and need volume.

Sean's favourite quote and the idea under his approach to setbacks. A loss is not fixed; how you look at it decides what you get from it. Reframing is the skill to build.

- The quote Sean returns to: when you change the way you look at things, things begin to change.
- Applied to losses: when you take an L, the important thing is to understand how to reframe it.
- The reframes he uses: look at your own part instead of blaming others, and ask what the loss is teaching you.

#### Pivot and adapt to thrive

When a strategy fails, you change direction instead of quitting. Shaun learned this in COVID, when his serviced accommodation, HMO and student strategies collapsed and he had to choose between going back to a job or finding a new model. Being able to pivot is what keeps you in business.

- In COVID his existing strategies failed, so he had to adapt, pivot and change.
- The decision in 2020 was binary: scrap the business and go back to the 9-to-5, or come out swinging and find a new strategy.
- He chose to find a new strategy, which turned out to be social housing.
- He calls this a huge entrepreneurial lesson: you have to be able to pivot and adapt to thrive in business.

#### Position yourself as an expert

Councils respond to people who sound certain and know what value they bring. If you present as someone still figuring it out, you will not get a reply. Speak with certainty and confidence.

- Do not position yourself as a time waster, as someone who does not know what they are doing, or as someone trying to figure it out
- Speak with certainty and confidence
- Help the council officer understand what value you bring to the table
- Result: they are more likely to be responsive and to email back faster

#### Protect your time like your revenue

Also known as: If it isn't high value, I don't do it

Shaun treats his hours the way he treats his income: nothing gets spent on things that do not pay. On his one free day, if a task was not high value it simply did not get done by him. When your time is scarce, this rule decides what you touch and what you hand off.

- Be extremely disciplined with the time you have. Every minute is intentional.
- Ask of every task: is this high value? If not, it does not get done by you.
- Guard your time with the same seriousness you guard your revenue. In Shaun's words, that is the difference.

#### Provide a safe space, quality accommodation and support

Also known as: The impact of social housing, Change lives, even save lives

Shaun's stated reason for loving social housing is the daily impact on people. Tenants arrive from every walk of life, some homeless, some sleeping in cars or on the street, and the job is to give them a safe space, good accommodation and real support. Hold this as the standard for what your houses are for.

- Tenants come from all walks of life; some are homeless, some are sleeping in their cars or in the streets
- What the business is there to provide: a safe space, quality accommodation and quality support
- Shaun says the business gets to genuinely change lives, and even save lives, on a daily basis
- Tenant stories in the video: a man evicted over rent arrears, a long-standing tenant now starting a cake business, a man told to sleep rough after the hospital was full, a man whose partner put him out when he lost his job

- Many of Shaun's landlords had bad experiences with social housing before he met them.
- If they truly believe in you because you have done the work of building rapport, the past will not matter. They are now dealing with you.

#### Recessions are where you grow the most

Also known as: Solve problems and create value in all seasons, Don't retract when times get hard

Recessions come round as part of the cycle, so plan to win in every season, not just the good ones. Your job as an entrepreneur is to solve problems and create value; do that well and you can thrive in a downturn. Pulling back out of fear is how people sabotage their own success, because the downturn is the moment with the most room to grow.

- Covid nearly wiped Shaun out. He adapted by looking at the trend and changing strategy.
- A great entrepreneurial trait: understand that you are there to solve problems and create value. Do that effectively and you thrive.
- Recessions are always coming as part of the cycle. Three responses: some people thrive, some spot the opportunities, some retract and pull back.
- Retracting out of fear sabotages your success, because a recession is the opportunity to grow the most.

#### Record everything: reporting and duty of care

Even though the nightly rate model has less compliance than other social housing models, you still carry a legal duty of care. Keep spreadsheets and compliance records for every issue so you can show the council what was reported and when.

- Keep track of your reporting: spreadsheets, compliance records, every issue logged
- Be ready for the council to ask: this was an issue, did you report it?
- You have a duty of care and a legal obligation to do things properly and record them
- This model has probably the least compliance among social housing models; others have higher requirements and more inspections, but your duty remains
- If it is a rent-to-rent, the landlord also wants the property kept to the best standard

- Reinvest heavily rather than spend
- Double down on what works: go back to agents, book more viewings, talk to more landlords
- Do not plan on spending the first profits on lifestyle

- Give them confidence that you are great for them and that they should do business with you
- The landlord in the video bought into the team's confidence and knowledge, not a discount

#### Same reward for lower work

Sean's tie-breaker between business models. If two routes pay the same and one takes less work, take the one with less work. It is why he left serviced accommodation for temporary accommodation.

- Compare the reward and the work of each option, not just the reward.
- If the reward is the same and the work is lower, choose the lower work. Sean's question: why would you not?
- Applied: serviced accommodation with its guests, cleans and 24/7 calls was not the level of work he wanted long term; temporary accommodation pays consistently with minimal operations.

#### Setbacks are feedback, not failure

Also known as: Fail your way to success, Yes is a destination, no is how you get there, Failure and success are in the same direction

Failure is not the opposite of success, it is on the way to it. Shaun used to picture himself in the middle with failure on one side and success on the other. The book Go for No showed him the real line: you, then failure, then success. Every setback is feedback you need to reach the goal.

- Source: the book Go for No, whose slogan is "yes is a destination, no is how you get there". He highly recommends it for entrepreneurs.
- Old mental picture: failure to the left, success to the right, you in the middle. New picture: you, then failure, then success, all in the same direction.
- Failure is part of the process and part of success. You have to fail your way to success.
- It is in the failure and the setbacks that you learn the most lessons (see the bicep curl analogy and the success is a lazy teacher card).

Success comes from turning up every day and doing the work whether or not you feel like it. Shaun says that if he relied on his emotions he would not show up half the time. He built the habit instead, so that his feelings and his actions are no longer connected.

Also known as: Transferable skills

What you learn in one business or job carries over to the next. Shaun points to his father, who took 20 years of skills from his security business into a food company he acquired. Shaun himself took sales and leadership skills from car dealerships into property.

- Shaun's case: he helped grow his father's food business with his sales background, then took car sales skills into rent to rent.

#### Solve a problem, get paid in proportion to the value

Also known as: You get paid in proportion to the problems you solve, Compensation follows value

Shaun frames the whole business as problem solving. There is a housing crisis, the contract winners need lots of houses, and you get paid in proportion to how much of that problem you solve. If you keep asking who has a problem you can fix, the money follows.

- Entrepreneurship is creating value and solving problems.
- If you solve a problem you get money, in proportion to the value you create.
- The problem right now: a massive housing crisis. Providers have won government-backed contracts and need a lot of houses.
- Your job: supply those houses, and you are good to go.

#### Speak to your tenants directly

Also known as: Hear the tenant's point of view, Speak to your customer

Your tenants are your customers, so ask them in person how they find the service and what you could improve. Shaun says you usually hear the provider's, landlord's or investor's view, and the only way to know what the customer thinks is to speak to them yourself. He spends a day walking his houses doing exactly that.

- Most of what you hear in the sector comes from the provider, the landlord or the investor. Make time to hear the tenant's point of view.
- Ask about the service, the accommodation, the staff and the house, and what could be done better.
- Shaun frames it as the beauty of business: you need to speak to your actual client, and in social housing the client is the tenant.
- The purpose is constant improvement: striving for perfection and for a better product and service. Speaking to tenants directly is, in his words, the only way to find out how.
- For newcomers to the sector, hearing tenants explains how it all works and what the tenants are like.

#### Speed wins the deal

Also known as: Velocity

When several providers want the same house, the one who gets the council inspector out first wins. Move the moment a landlord shows interest. In the video another agent had enquired first but lost because they were slow to call the council.

- Call the council the same day and ask when a surveyor can come; take any cancellation slot
- Call the landlord immediately to fix the time
- Viewing first can earn you first refusal from the landlord
- Time is of the essence from the viewing to the keys; the Dave deal went from cold viewing to keys in under 24 hours

#### Spread across areas and councils

Do not build your whole business on one council or one town. Working across several areas and councils spreads your risk. The author recommends it strongly.

- You can scale within one area, but having multiple areas and multiple councils is a great way of mitigating risk
- The area itself does not matter; go where the demand is

#### Stack monthly income, don't chase big profit per deal

Shaun tells students not to obsess over a huge margin on each property. On the AP model every new deal adds its monthly income on top of what you already earn, so modest margins across many properties grow into a large, steadily rising income.

- It is not about making a massive profit on every single property.
- Every time you add a property, its money goes on top of what you already earn, on a monthly basis.
- So you are consistently growing.
- The student says this was the massive appeal for him.

- Snowball effect: more confidence and a better understanding of what you are doing
- Social proof: something tangible to show landlords, agents and councils, showing how you helped the landlord, the tenant and the council

#### Success is a feeling, not a finish line

Also known as: Living life on your own terms

Sean used to picture success as a big house, a flashy car and holidays whenever he liked. Getting them changed nothing. He now says success is not a finish line you cross but a feeling: living on your own terms, providing for your family and team, and being accountable for a positive impact.

What success feels like to Sean now:
- Being able to live life on his own terms.
- Being able to provide for his family and for his team.
- Being accountable and responsible, and knowing the work makes a positive impact on the community. His organisation houses hundreds of tenants and he describes it as saving and changing lives daily.
- Creating reliable, stable careers where people can thrive and hit their own goals.

What it is not: the house, the car, the holidays. Those arrive, the novelty wears off within months and life goes on.

- Success teaches little. Failure and pivots teach a lot.
- Before COVID: great success, no learning. During the pivot to social housing: six months with more lessons and growth than in years.
- When you get to the other side of a setback, you are a different person as a result.

Running a business is lonely, and people who do not run one cannot really help you through it. Sean says being part of a community of entrepreneurs and high performers is crucial, and will move you further and faster than figuring it out alone.

- Loneliness in entrepreneurship is real. Sean felt it while running his business.
- Talking to family and friends is sometimes not the solution: if they do not run a business they will not understand how to help you navigate the problems.
- What works: like-minded people who understand business, so you can work through hardship, cash flow issues and growing pains together.

- His strongest statement: being surrounded by other high performers is crucial to your success and will propel it further and quicker than suffering in silence.

#### Take massive, imperfect, relentless action

Shaun's own motto, and his cure for waiting until everything is ready. Massive: go big. Imperfect: start with what you have, where you are and who you know. Relentless: play to win, not to take part. The energy of someone obsessed is also what makes others want to do business with you.

- Massive: the action needs to be big. There is no point going small; go big or go bigger.
- Imperfect: entrepreneurs, new and old, like to have all their ducks in a row before they act. Things are never going to be perfect, and if you wait for the perfect time you will wait forever. Take action with what you have, where you are, with who you know and the knowledge you have.
- Relentless: there is no point playing the game if you do not want to win it. The point is to win, not to take part, and winning takes relentlessness and obsession. Grant Cardone: "Be obsessed or be average." Live and breathe your business.
- Why it pays: people want to work with you and do business with you when they can see your energy and that you are a true student of the game, rather than someone blase, laid back and not switched on.
- Speed matters much more than perfection.

#### Take responsibility for your own success

Also known as: No excuses mentality, Results or excuses, never both, Success is your duty, Take responsibility and you keep the power, It's never the team, it's my leadership

Everything that happens in your business is your fault, including what your team does. Blaming someone else hands them the power and leaves you unable to fix the problem. Shaun asks what he could have done differently instead, fixes it fast and keeps moving.

- Everything that happens in the business is the owner's fault. Do not blame the team or the staff. There is always something you could have done to prevent it.
- Why: when you blame people you give the power to them, and if someone else has the power you have no control to fix the situation.
- What to do instead: put the power back in your hands. Say: this is what happened, this is the situation, what could I have done differently? This lets you fix problems quickly and keep it moving.
- Rule of thumb: you can have results or excuses, never both.
- The final tip of the talk is the same idea: develop a no excuses mentality. Success is your duty. No one else is responsible, and if you fail it is only your fault. No more excuses, only action.

- No need for the latest tech or top interior design.
- Requirement: fit for purpose and suitable for the council.
- The property becomes someone's home. The tenant is responsible for furnishing it and getting it the way they like it.
- Business effect: upfront set-up costs are substantially lower than serviced accommodation.

- Shaun's rule: someone who stays in their home city their whole life becomes complacent and comfortable, often subconsciously. There is no growth in your comfort zone.

#### You don't need more time, you need focused hours

Also known as: Time is not the real problem, The I-don't-have-time myth, Focused hours, not free hours

The most common excuse Shaun hears is "I don't have time". He says it really means you have not yet learned how to use the time you have. You do not need endless free hours to build a rent-to-rent temporary accommodation business. You need a few focused hours and the structure and leverage to make them count.

- The excuse "I don't have time" really means "I haven't yet learned how to manage my time".
- The idea that you need loads of free time to start in property is a myth. Time has never been the real problem. The real problem is lack of structure and lack of leverage.
- You do not need more hours. You need better use of the hours you already have. Shaun calls this one of the biggest mindset shifts you need to make.
- Instead of more time you need four things:
  1. Clarity
  2. Structure
  3. Leverage
  4. The discipline to stop giving energy to low value tasks that do not move the needle
- If you do that, you can build the business around any job, any lifestyle and any responsibility, no matter how busy you think you are.

**Concepts**

#### Actual figures, not projected figures

Also known as: Council rates agreed in advance, Financial security through consistency

A serviced accommodation deal is priced on what you hope to earn. A temporary accommodation deal is priced on rates the council agrees in advance. Sean values knowing exactly what each property pays for the next 12 months, because it lets him budget and plan.

- Serviced accommodation: when you close a property today you are working on projected figures, not actual figures.
- Temporary accommodation: the council gives the rates in advance, so when Sean closes a property he knows exactly what he will receive for the next 12 months, consistent every single month.
- Why it matters to him: financial security. He wants to budget and plan and know exactly what he is getting for a property.

#### Back against the wall

Also known as: Low basic, high commission

Status: needs review

In car sales Shaun had a very low basic wage and earned mostly commission. Because the basic did not even cover his bills, every month he had to deliver results or go unpaid. He credits that pressure with building the drive he later used in business.

- Structure: very low basic salary plus high commission.
- The basic wage was lower than his monthly bills, so each month he had to hit results to get paid.
- Effect: every single month his back was against the wall, which forced him to deliver.

#### Becoming a provider directly with the council

Also known as: Direct council route, Government gateway

Instead of partnering with a provider, you can be the provider yourself. Some councils let you call and sign up directly; others go through the government gateway, where Contracts Finder is the way in. Going direct means the risk and the tenant management sit with you, though temporary accommodation needs little management and maintenance can be outsourced.

Two routes into social housing:
1. Partner with existing providers, found on Contracts Finder or elsewhere.
2. Become a provider directly with a council.

How to go direct:
- Certain councils: just call them and become a provider.
- Certain councils go through the government gateway; there, Contracts Finder is your best bet.
- Shaun did this with Sandhu on the challenge: they became a provider directly with Luton Council.

Two things to know about going direct:
1. The risk is all on you, not on a provider.
2. You are the provider, so you manage the tenant.

What management involves:
- Temporary accommodation does not take much: a family lives there and only contacts you if there is an issue with the property.
- Even that can be outsourced to a maintenance company.

- The reframe: the job pays a wage, but the same 60 hours put into the business would bring a much higher opportunity and reward, so the job is a cost.

#### Cold calling is the highest level of sales

Status: needs review

Cold calling means selling to someone who does not know you or your product and was not thinking of buying. Shaun calls it the highest level of sales, because you must get the person to like you, listen and buy, all from nothing. Warm inbound leads are far easier since the person is already half interested.

Why cold calling is the hardest form of selling, in Shaun's words:
1. The person does not know who you are.
2. The person does not know what the product is.
3. You have to convince them to like you personally, listen to you and give you the time of day.
4. You then have to convince them to buy something they were not thinking about buying.

By contrast, with inbound leads the lead is already warm and half interested; your job is just to direct them to the sale and to the right product.

Shaun's experience: working for his father he did nothing but cold calling, going to different shops. He found it really hard but enjoyed it, and says it teaches you a lot about business.

Sources:
- R-YT-PKg8fPvSkfw (06:01)

#### Company let

Also known as: Company lets, Guaranteed rent, What the company offers the landlord, Agents refuse company lets through lack of education, Company let objection, Leasing to a company beats renting to a tenant, Lease to a company, not a tenant, Commercial lease, not an AST, Why rent-to-rent is legal, AST, Assured shorthold tenancy agreement

Status: needs review

A company let means the tenancy is in your company's name, not the occupant's, so your company is responsible for the property. For the landlord it means you take the tenant, the maintenance and the rent risk off their hands under one contract. Agents will ask whether you "do company lets", and their answer decides whether you can work with them; big chains and some independents restrict it.

- What it is: you are not renting directly to the tenant. You rent to the company, and the company is responsible. Shaun calls this the game changer for the landlord.
- What the landlord gets:
  - You manage the tenant.
  - You manage the maintenance, including on the way in.
  - You guarantee the rent, paid in full even if the house sits empty for 12 months.
  - You sign a contract and both sides are locked in for the whole term.
- The question agents ask: "Do you guys do company lets?"
- Restrictions you will meet:
  - Nationwide brands (Connells) can only do company lets when a business rents for its own employees.
  - Some agents have changed their rules: if the company is a letting agent or is subletting, they will not do it.
  - In the end the landlord decides what kind of people they accept, including whether a company can be the tenant.

Notes: The transcript writes 'company less'; the speaker means company lets.

You can earn from a property without owning it. The idea is to control an asset that produces cash flow, for example by leasing it from a landlord and leasing it on to a council. This is what makes a low-cash start possible.

- Owning is not the only route. Controlling an asset that produces cash flow also makes money.

#### Deal sourcing

Also known as: A year of deal sourcing in 2020

Deal sourcing means finding investors, finding and analysing property deals, and selling those deals to the investors for a fee. It is cheap to set up and the lowest-risk of the three strategies, but you have to keep finding new investors and new deals every month.

- Set-up cost: about £3,000 for compliance.
- Earnings: around £3,000 to £5,000 per deal on average.
- The work: go out every day and find investors. Then find the properties, analyse the deals and make sure they stack up. Then sell the deal to the investor.
- Risk: the lowest of the three. You are not committing to anything long term. Once the investor accepts the deal it is their responsibility.
- Downside: you start every month on a blank canvas, chasing investors and deals again.
- Needs a proper contract with clauses so the investor cannot come back later and say the deal does not work.

People worry about what happens when the contract on a rented property runs out. The conditions on your side:
- You have been doing a good job
- You have not been causing any hassle

#### Every council works differently

There is no single rulebook. Some councils find the tenants for you, others expect you to find them. Some place one family in a house, others want individual rooms in an HMO. Furnishing rules change too. You have to learn how each council you work with operates.

- Tenant sourcing: some councils find the tenants for you; with others you find the tenants yourself
- Occupancy: some councils place one family in a house; some run an HMO setup with individual rooms
- Furnishing: some councils require a full set of bedroom furniture; some only ask for a bed and expect the tenant to provide the rest

#### Hot providers

Also known as: Providers that are looking to scale

A hot provider is one that has just won a government-backed contract and now has to fill it. They have the funding and a deadline, so they are far more likely to want your properties than a random name from Google. Finding hot providers is the whole point of the Contracts Finder method.

- Definition: a provider that is actively looking to scale, typically because it has just closed a government-backed contract
- Why they are hot: the contract has an end date, so they want to fulfil it quickly; they need a lot of properties; the funding is already there
- Where to find them: awarded contracts on Contracts Finder (gov.uk/contractsfinder)
- What to do with them: contact them, find out whether they are looking to scale, and offer your properties as the solution

#### JV partnership versus investor debt

Also known as: Why JVs beat borrowed finance

People shy away from joint ventures because they hate giving away half the profit. The hosts flip it: in a JV you never have to pay anyone back, expectations are set from the start, and you can do far more deals with far less risk. The trade-off is that a bad partner is very stressful.

- With a JV partner you split the profit; their money is tied up in the deal, but there is no repayment deadline hanging over you because the expectation is set at the start.
- With borrowed finance you keep the profit but must repay on a date whether or not the project is done.
- Volume argument: splitting profit but doing many more deals beats keeping all the profit on a few.
- Risk argument: much less risk because there is no finance to pay back.
- Downside: a bad JV partner is very stressful. Luigi says about 95% of his partners have been amazing and he has learned a lot.

Notes: Luigi buys with JV partners; Shaun makes the volume and risk argument.

- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (28:00)

Also known as: What people will remember

Most people think of legacy as the assets and money you leave behind. Shaun thinks of it as the impact you had on people. No one will remember your business or your money, but they will remember the private conversations, the wisdom, the contact you made for them and the help you gave off camera.

- The common view: legacy means your assets and your money.
- Shaun's view: when you are gone nobody remembers the business or the money you had. They remember the impact you had.
- Impact looks like: private conversations with people, things done off camera as well as on camera to help someone and change their life, a piece of wisdom, a gem you give them, a contact you make for them.

#### LHA rate plus Universal Credit top-up

Also known as: LHA rate, Local housing rate

Under the A model you receive roughly the LHA rate, sometimes slightly more, and the tenant pays a top-up from their Universal Credit. The top-up is where your risk sits. It depends on the tenant managing their money and knowing what is due when.

- You receive usually the LHA rate or a little above it
- The tenant pays a top-up out of their Universal Credit
- Risk: the tenant must understand how to manage their income, what the bills are and the dates they must be paid
- Mitigation: collect by direct debit through a portal that flags failures and cancellations, and ask the council what support exists if the tenant defers

Notes: The author uses LHA as a known term and does not expand it.

#### Local Authority Leasing

Also known as: LAL, Back-to-back leasing

You take a property and lease it straight to the council, which pays you a guaranteed rent for several years. There is nothing to manage day to day, so you get paid wherever you are. Shaun calls this one of his favourite strategies for the financial security it gives.

- How it works: get a property and lease it directly to the council.
- Rent: guaranteed every single month, paid directly by the council.
- Term: Shaun says 3 to 5 years at first, and in his recap 3 to 6 years, sometimes even 7 or 10 years.
- Operations: zero. You can be anywhere in the world and still get paid consistently.
- Upfront incentive: depending on the scheme, the council sometimes pays 1, 2 or even 3 months rent upfront, then rents the property from you for the rest of the term.
- It is a government backed scheme, which is where Shaun gets his sense of financial security.
- Also known as back-to-back leasing: you lease the property straight back out to the council, with no operations in between.

Notes: The transcript renders the abbreviation as 'L'; from context it is the abbreviation of local authority leasing. Lease lengths are given inconsistently in the chunk: 3 to 5 years at first, then 3 to 6 and up to 10.

#### Motivation comes in bursts, discipline fills the gaps

Motivation is not steady. It shows up in bursts, usually at the start of something new or when things go well, then it fades. That is when discipline and habit have to take over. Build the habit of showing up while you are motivated so it is there when you are not.

- Motivation comes in bursts, typically at the start of something new or when things are going well.
- When motivation fades, discipline needs to occur.
- So show up regardless of how you feel and build the habits now, so that when motivation fades you still show up.
- Discipline is hard to build outside your purpose (see the purpose card).
- Everyone feels different levels and bursts of motivation at different times. Winners are not gifted with a motivated personality; they show up regardless.

A deal where you put no money in: no deposit and no rent paid upfront. Because your investment is zero, any profit is an infinite return. Michael has never paid a deposit across his nine properties.

- No deposit and no upfront rent
- Maintenance may be split between you and the landlord
- With zero money in, the return on investment is infinite
- Michael's fourth property: no money down, £500 a month passive profit
- Michael has paid no deposit on any of his properties; Shaun has paid a deposit on about three of his

#### One foot in front of the other, not Mount Everest

Also known as: Don't try to climb Mount Everest, Figure it out along the way

Shaun's picture for progress: do not stare at the whole mountain, just take the next step. You cannot plan the full climb because the future is unknown. Keep moving one step at a time and things work themselves out as you go.

- Do not try to climb Mount Everest in one go; that is not possible when nobody can see the future.
- Put one foot in front of the other and figure it out along the way.
- Do not slow down while you figure it out.
- Things work themselves out as you go along.

#### People need to believe you believe

Also known as: Enthusiasm sells, passion sells, Conviction

A landlord does not have to be convinced by your argument first. They have to be convinced that you are convinced. Sean says that when people believe you believe in what you are saying, you come across with conviction, and passion and enthusiasm are what sell.

- People do not need to believe in what you are saying. People need to believe that you believe in what you are saying. Sean calls it a subtle difference.
- When they believe you believe, you come across with conviction: passionate, enthusiastic.
- Enthusiasm sells, passion sells. Getting that across is very important.
- This is something you have to work on yourself; it cannot be scripted for you.

#### Private Sector Leasing

Instead of leasing to the council itself, you lease your property to a housing association or to a provider the council has already approved. Many councils only work through their approved providers, so this is often the way in when the council will not take you on directly.

- Who you lease to: housing associations and council approved providers.
- Why it matters: for temporary and emergency accommodation, certain councils only work with their approved providers and will not take on a new provider directly.
- Like Local Authority Leasing, this is a back-to-back lease with zero operations: you lease the property out to the provider and they manage the tenant.
- If the council will not take new providers, ask who its current approved providers are and approach them. That question has its own card.

#### Productive vs busy

Also known as: Moving the needle, Busy as a disguise for procrastination, Start with high income generating tasks, Move the needle, Busy is not productive, Grinding 24/7 is not a badge of honour, Being busy, not productive

Status: needs review

Being busy is not the same as being productive. Most people spend their time on things that do not move the business forward and call it work. Shaun's test is one question: does this move the needle? If not, it is procrastination in disguise.

- There is a difference between being productive and being busy. People spend a lot of time doing things that do not move the needle on their business.
- Being busy can be procrastination in disguise.
- As the CEO, work on high value tasks that move the needle, and outsource, delegate and automate the low value tasks (see let go to grow).
- The key is not how many hours you work, it is where you work.
- Hold yourself accountable to your productivity: do not just be busy, be productive.

#### Rent to rent

Also known as: Rent to rent strategy, Three ways to find rent to rent deals, How to get involved in rent to rent, Rent-to-rent, Rent to rent social housing, Rent to social housing, Rent to rent vs rent to social housing: provider first, Provider first, Two different strategies, Setting up a rent to social housing deal, A typical deal, How rent to rent works, Rent to rent is a low barrier to entry, Start with what you have, Getting stung by a rent to rent serviced accommodation deal, Rent to rent Airbnb deal, Rent-to-rent social housing from start to finish, The social housing model in simple terms, Rent-to-social housing, Control, don't own, Rent-to-rent is commercial leasing, Commercial leasing, Commercial lease agreement, Company let agreement, Premier Inn does rent-to-rent, Social housing is the rent-to-rent sweet spot, Zero tenant marketing

Status: needs review

Rent to rent means you lease a property from a landlord on a commercial lease or company let, pay them a guaranteed rent, and then use the property for your own business. Here that business is leasing it on to the council for temporary accommodation. It lets you build cash flow quickly without buying property.

1. Sign a commercial lease or company let with a landlord directly.
2. Offer the landlord a guaranteed rent.
3. Use the property for your own purpose.
4. In this case, lease it back out to the council and run temporary accommodation in it.

- Shaun has used rent to rent for 6 and a half years and has over 90 rent to rent social housing properties.
- He presents it as the way to build cash flow very quickly and acquire properties for the strategy.

#### Serviced accommodation

Also known as: SA, Service accommodation, Serviced accommodation runs in peaks and troughs, COVID killed the serviced accommodation business

Serviced accommodation means letting a property for short stays, Airbnb style. It costs the most to set up because you compete on interior design and a unique selling point, and the earning potential is high in busy periods. The author ran it for three years and then stopped.

- Start-up cost: around £7,000 to £15,000.
- Why the cost is higher: there are lots of very basic Airbnbs. The ones making money are unique, with great interior design. You have to spend to get it up to standard and think about your USP.
- Upside: in a prime location during a special event you can charge three or even four times the normal amount.
- Downside: peaks and troughs. Booked out in summer, horrendous in winter, and you usually hold a rent-to-rent lease with no guarantee of bookings.
- The author's verdict: great if you love hospitality. He left it after COVID for a recession-proof model.

#### Supported versus temporary accommodation

Also known as: Why Shaun scales temporary accommodation nationwide, Supported accommodation, Social housing, Temporary to permanent accommodation, Two social housing strategies, Temporary accommodation, Supported living vs supported accommodation, Choose temporary accommodation, Hands down temporary accommodation, Temporary accommodation needs no CQC or Ofsted, No care, support or supervision, Rent-to-rent temporary accommodation, TA, Temporary accommodation provider, You cover the maintenance in temporary accommodation deals

Status: needs review

Shaun runs over 90 rent-to-rent social housing deals and splits them into supported and temporary accommodation. Supported accommodation pays well but has many moving parts, needs people on the ground and brings council audits. Temporary accommodation has no support element once tenants move in, so it is the model he is scaling nationwide.

- Supported accommodation: Shaun runs it specifically in Birmingham. Very lucrative if done correctly, but a lot of turning cogs: you need many people on the ground, property audits, and working closely with the council can be daunting.
- Scaling supported accommodation nationwide would mean a very large team and a lot of headache; Shaun judges it not worth the reward.
- Temporary accommodation: once the tenant is in there is no support element. It is 100% worth the reward and can be scaled with many different councils around the UK.
- Shaun's plan changed from national expansion via supported accommodation to national expansion via temporary accommodation.

#### Targeted marketing

Sean calls the Contracts Finder approach targeted marketing. You are not calling random names; you are calling providers who have a contract to fill and money to pay for it. That is why it closes back-to-back leases faster than cold searching.

- Random marketing: Googling providers and calling anyone you find
- Targeted marketing: calling providers who have just been awarded a contract, so you know they need properties
- Result: you are more likely to close a back-to-back lease with the provider
- Every contract on Contracts Finder is a batch of warm leads (eight suppliers on one contract means eight leads straight away)

- R-YT-aRcmGZIV7Y4 (07:01)

#### Tenant marketing: the hidden cost of a strategy

With Airbnb and working-professional HMOs you have to go out and find your tenants, and that never stops. In supported accommodation most tenants come to you through council and homeless shelter referrals. In temporary accommodation there is no tenant marketing at all. Shaun sees this as the main reason to leave Airbnb and HMO behind.

- Airbnb and working professionals: problem number one is tenant marketing. You have to market to find the tenants.
- Supported accommodation: a little marketing at times, but mostly the tenants come through homeless shelters and the council, who send referrals.
- Temporary accommodation: zero tenant marketing, which is why he calls it his favourite and the most passive strategy.
- He is handing back all his rent-to-rent Airbnbs to get rid of the stress.

#### The A model

Also known as: The AP model, Assured periodic tenancies, AP model, Rent to social housing, How the AP model works in a nutshell, The AP model deal structure, Pick the AP model for reliability, AP model versus deal sourcing, Rent to rent, Rent-to-rent lease

Status: needs review

A growing model where single occupants are housed in an HMO and the rent is paid directly by DWP rather than from the council's own budget. Councils like it because it protects their funds while meeting their duty. It carries more risk and more tenant management than the nightly rate, but the author says it tends to make more money.

- Who: single occupants
- Where: usually an HMO, each tenant with a lockable bedroom, shared facilities, fire regulations in place
- Payer: housing benefit paid directly by DWP, not from the council's pot of money
- Rent: usually the LHA rate or a little above, plus a tenant top-up from Universal Credit
- Incentives from many councils: a cash incentive, a deposit, sometimes two to three months of rent up front
- Workload: more tenants and characters to manage, more housing management than one family
- Risk: the tenant's top-up depends on them managing their income
- Payoff: tends to make more money despite the risk

Notes: The author does not expand the abbreviations LHA or DWP in this talk.

#### The blank canvas problem of serviced accommodation

Also known as: Why temp accommodation beats serviced accommodation, Serviced accommodation, SA, Service accommodation, The trashed apartment: why serviced accommodation wasn't for him, Serviced accommodation: peaks and troughs, Airbnb seasonality

Serviced accommodation starts from zero every month: no bookings until you fill them, plus check-ins, reviews, cleaning, linen, parties and trashed houses. Shaun compares it to his old car-sales job of low basic and high commission. Temporary accommodation gives him the opposite: he knows what is coming in months ahead and can plan.

- Serviced accommodation workload: guests checking in and out constantly, hospitality reviews, cleaning, changing linen, parties, people trashing the house.
- Income pattern: a blank canvas every month. In March you look at April and May and see no bookings yet.
- Shaun's car-sales comparison: very low basic, high commission, and every month you start from zero again.
- Temporary accommodation: once the property is on board there is little to do, and the income is stable month after month, so you can plan.
- Overall Shaun rates it a lot less risky.

#### The council lease scheme with upfront incentive

Also known as: Leasing to the council, Council incentives replacing nightly rates, Upfront incentive, Longer-form temporary accommodation, Council incentives, Incentive payments, Cash up front from councils

Temporary accommodation is expensive for councils, so some are moving away from nightly rates. Instead they pay two to three months' rent upfront as an incentive and lease the property from you for up to five years or more. The tenant pays a top-up from housing benefit.

- Why: the temporary accommodation sector is very costly for councils, and some are changing their schemes.
- Incentive: two to three months' rent paid upfront.
- Term: the council leases the property off you for up to five years, sometimes longer.
- Tenant top-up: the tenant pays a top-up through housing benefits on top of what the council pays you.
- Shaun's mentees run this version across the UK and it works well.

#### The curse of the entrepreneur

Also known as: Never feeling like you're doing enough

Ambitious people carry a constant feeling that they are not doing enough. Shaun calls it the curse of being an entrepreneur. Knowing it is a built-in feeling, not a true signal, helps you resist chasing every new idea.

- The feeling shows up even when things are going well; Shaun says he sometimes sits in bed thinking he needs to do more, more, more.
- It makes it hard to find balance and to decide where to spend your time.
- Shaun's answer to it: keep doing what is doing well rather than adding more.

Sources:
- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (07:00)

#### The journey of a £10 note

Also known as: How money moves, Money is always moving

Follow one £10 note and you see that money never sits still: it goes from you to a shop, to the shop's supplier, to a worker, and finally to a bank. The lesson is that money is always moving, and the question is whether it moves through you or past you.

The journey as the author lays it out:
1. You hold £10.
2. It goes to a shop.
3. The shop passes it to a supplier.
4. The supplier pays a worker.
5. Finally it goes to the bank.

What it teaches: money is always in motion. Ask yourself whether you are watching it move from the outside or standing in the middle so that it passes through you.

Sources:
- R-YT-OvrW406xkGs (02:00)

#### The nightly-rate scheme

Also known as: Nightly rate temporary accommodation, Nightly rate scheme, Temporary accommodation, TA, Emergency housing, Nightly rate temporary accommodation model, TA core model, Nightly rate model, Emergency accommodation, Rent-to-council

Status: needs review

The simplest temporary accommodation deal. The council places a tenant and pays you a nightly rate you negotiate, and the profit can be £500 to £1,500 a month per property after all costs. Operations are minimal, so the model runs from anywhere; Shaun has mentees doing it from America and Paris.

- The council places a tenant and pays a nightly rate; you negotiate that rate.
- Profit: roughly £500 to £1,500 per month after all costs.
- Operations are very minimal, which is why it scales nationwide and can be run remotely, even from Dubai.
- Everything can be outsourced; mentees in America and Paris run it.
- Weakness: there is no contract. It is done on a nightly basis and you invoice the council at the end of each month.

#### The PRS ombudsman scheme

Also known as: PRS, Join the PRS and use it as a selling point, Public redress scheme, Ombudsman membership

The PRS is the ombudsman scheme the author belongs to. Membership shows councils you are a proper business that will stick to its word, and gives tenants, landlords and third parties an adjudicator if something goes wrong. For a new company it buys peace of mind.

- You need to be part of an ombudsman scheme. The author uses the PRS.
- Other ombudsman services exist.
- What it signals: you are a good business, willing to provide a service and stick to your word.
- What it does: if that does not happen, tenants, landlords and third parties can go to an adjudicator who looks at the situation and solves the problem.
- Why it matters: it gives everyone peace of mind, especially when you are a new company.

#### The rhythm of success

Successful people have a rhythm to how they work, move and solve problems. Shaun says it is the biggest thing a newcomer picks up from shadowing him: how quickly you deal with a challenge, how you approach a situation and how you think on your feet.

- There is a rhythm to success. People who have achieved something great share it.
- You see it in: the way they work, the way they move, how quickly they deal with challenges, how they approach situations, how they solve problems, how they create value, how they think on their feet.
- It is learned by watching and doing alongside someone who has it, which is why day one was shadowing.

#### The snowball effect

Also known as: Each deal closes the next, Get agents on side and property flows to you, Snowball effect

Your first deal is the hardest. Every deal you close gives you standing to close the next one, because you can show landlords the properties you already run. Momentum builds from there.

- The first deal helps you close the second; the second helps close the next, and so on
- Each closed deal gives you a leg to stand on with the next landlord
- Existing properties become your proof when you pitch new ones
- Michael went from zero to nine deals in three months on this effect

#### The three C's

Also known as: Courage, creativity, commitment

A Grant Cardone quote Shaun uses as his answer to "I need money first". You do not need money to make money; you need courage, creativity and commitment. It is the mindset behind every no-money strategy in the video.

- Courage: go out there and actually get it done. Put yourself forward.
- Creativity: find a structure that works without capital instead of waiting to save.
- Commitment: the third C.
- Shaun says the quote made him realise he needed to be more forefront and remove his limitations, because every limitation is self-imposed.

Shaun measures the business by lives changed, not just income. One student changing his own life has housed over 80 tenants; across the whole community the number is probably in the thousands.

- The student changed his own life and, through his properties, over 80 tenants' lives.
- Across the academy community, Shaun estimates the effect reaches into the thousands.

#### Upfront incentive per room

Also known as: Cash incentive, Incentive, The incentive is paid again on every move-out, Repeat incentive

When you hand a property to the council or provider under the AP model, they pay you a lump sum per room before any rent comes in. Shaun calls it the biggest game changer of the strategy. It is separate from your monthly profit and solves the cash flow problem most new businesses have.

- On an HMO basis: about £600 to £1,000 per room, depending on where you are based.
- Paid upfront, separate from the monthly income.
- Examples from the video: seven-bed at £600 per room = £4,200; four-bed at £600 per room = £2,400; two-bed family let = £1,100.
- It is paid again each time somebody moves out (see the separate card).
- Use: keep it separate from monthly earnings, pay bills with some of it, or roll it into the next property.

Notes: Amounts depend on the area; the figures here are from one council in Greater Manchester.
