#### Value perception

Also known as: Perception of value, Value creation

What people pay is driven by what they believe something is worth, not just by what it does. Brands build that belief on purpose through marketing and design. Once you see this, you can raise the price of a simple product without changing what it does.

- Two watches both tell the time. One sells for about £50, the other from £10,000. The difference is perception.
- Rolex has created the perception that its watch is a luxurious item, that people want it, and that it is worth a lot of money.
- They built that perception through marketing and clever design.
- Lesson three in one line: value perception is key, make people want to give you their money.
- The author shows two ways to create the same effect with a simple product (see the lemonade stand).

#### What people don't see behind closed doors

Also known as: Keeping the cogs turning, People only see the end product

Social media shows the lifestyle of a property investor. It does not show the work behind closed doors that keeps the business running. The speakers want you to know that this hidden part is a lot, and that you need resilience to carry it.

- What people see: the lifestyle, and whatever else they want to see on social media.
- What they do not see: what the operators have to do behind closed doors to keep the cogs turning and run the business successfully. The speaker says it is a lot.
- Both speakers wish they could show more of the real stuff that goes on, but confidentiality stops them.
- What it demands of you: resilience, and a way of pouring back into yourself so you do not burn out.

#### Whatever you focus on grows

Also known as: Self-fulfilling prophecy, Law of attraction

What you give your attention to is what expands in your life. Focus on the negative and you get more negativity; think negatively and you attract negative things. So point your attention at what you want, not at what you do not want.

- Applies to business: the thing you focus on most is what grows.
- Applies to mindset: focusing on the negative brings more negativity in. Shaun calls it a self-fulfilling prophecy.
- Luigi's takeaway from the conversation: focus on what you want rather than what you do not want, because day-to-day noise makes it easy to lose sight of that.

#### Work on the business, not in the business

Also known as: Working on the business as opposed to in the business, Let go to grow, On the business, not in it, Leverage, Replace yourself in the business, Work on the business, not in it

Working in the business means doing the daily tasks yourself. Working on the business means managing it, improving it and steering it. Sean found the second one a much more productive way to grow, and it only becomes possible once you outsource.

- Working in the business: running around doing the jobs yourself.
- Working on the business: managing the business, coaching and empowering the team, tracking whether things move the needle.
- How you get there: outsource and leverage the lower-value jobs first. See the £500 per hour rule.
- Sean's verdict: a much better, much more productive way to grow.

**Techniques**

#### Ask the council for more after year one

Your rent to the landlord is fixed for three years, but the council rate is not. After twelve months go back and ask for more. Any increase goes straight to your profit in years two and three.

- Fixed cost: the landlord's rent under the 36-month lease
- After one year, ask the council to raise its payment
- The £500 monthly profit rises in years two and three by whatever increase you secure

Sources:
- R-YT-HJ5NOvSeCDA (1:12:00)

#### Avoid paying deposits

Also known as: Deposits are dead money

Status: needs review

Shaun hates paying deposits to landlords: the money is dead and it piles up fast across many properties. The student negotiates them away. It comes down to how well you get on with the landlord and how you word the ask.

- Deposits are dead money and accumulate very quickly as you scale.
- Example: someone Shaun spoke to had nearly £30,000 tied up in deposits because they did not realise avoiding them was an option.
- How: it depends on how well you get on with people, how you put yourself across, and the wording. Worded in the right way, landlords will work with you.

#### Best case, worst case, likely case

Also known as: Quick analysis, Worst case scenario check

A fast way to make a big decision without overthinking it. Write down the best case, the worst case and the likely case. When you look at the worst case on paper it is usually not that bad, and that is what lets you decide and commit. Shaun used it to hand in his notice on the spot.

1. Do the analysis there and then. Do not let the decision drag.
2. Write down three answers: What is the best case? What is the worst case? What is the likely case?
3. Look hard at the worst case and ask whether it is really fatal. In Shaun's words, it is not the case of going to die.
4. If the worst case is survivable, make the decision and commit to it quickly.
- Why it works: overthinking leads to procrastination and no decision. Seeing the worst case written down takes the fear out of it.
- His example: sitting at work after 18 months in property, he realised being there was costing him money. Worst case: the business fails and he goes back and gets another job in the trade. Not death, not homelessness. That was enough to decide immediately and hand in his notice.

Notes: The transcript says the worst case was getting a job again "in the multi-trade"; he most likely means the motor trade.

Sources:
- R-YT-O1krmmSq5Zo (05:00)
- R-YT-O1krmmSq5Zo (06:01)

#### Build a solid morning routine

Start each day with something that gives you energy so the day is set up for success. Shaun's options are a walk, the gym, a book or something motivational to listen to, and he does all of them. The routine matters; the exact hour does not.

- Build a solid morning routine. Shaun calls it key: set your day up for success.
- Fill it with things that generate energy. His list: go for a walk, go to the gym, read a book, listen to something motivational. He loves doing all of the above.
- You do not need to get up at 5 a.m. to do this (see the 5 a.m. myth card).

#### Build relationships with agents

Also known as: Off market deals through agents, Partnering with estate agents, Partner with agents, Build agent relationships face to face, Estate agents: relationship first, then WhatsApp, Off-market deals through agents, Winning a letting agent over, Getting property through letting agents

Instead of hunting listings online, go to the area you want to work in and walk into every letting agent. Get to know them. Once the relationship is there, agents send you properties before they ever reach the portals, so you are not competing with everyone else.

1. Pick the city or area you want to scale into.
2. Rather than searching Rightmove for that area, physically go there.
3. Visit every single agent in the area and start forming a relationship.
4. Build on that relationship over time; aim for long lasting, sustainable relationships.
5. Agents then start sending you off market deals, which are easier to close than listed properties.

Shaun says this has been an absolute game changer for him over 6 and a half years and is one of his best methods to close deals quickly.

Also known as: One hour a day finding providers, Daily provider outreach hour

Make the search a daily habit, or hand it to someone on your team. New contracts get awarded all the time, and the first person to call a newly funded provider gets the partnership. A few hours a day of this could mean several deals in a month.

1. Look at Contracts Finder every day, not once.
2. Outsource or leverage: get someone in your team to run the search for you.
3. When a provider has just closed a contract, contact them immediately and offer to partner.
4. Sean's estimate: spending a few hours a day on this could produce quite a few closed deals within a month.

The search itself is quick: under five minutes found over 40 providers.

#### Close landlords by solving their problem, not pitching

Do not walk in with a pitch. Sit down, learn what is worrying this landlord, then show how your service removes those worries. The author says this is the only way a close becomes frictionless.

1. Do not pitch aimlessly; the author calls that the fastest way to lose a deal
2. Sit down and understand the problems the landlord faces
3. Typical problems: uncertainty, the Renters' Rights Bill, periodic tenancies, the many rights tenants have in the UK
4. Show that your product and service is the solution: no voids, no daily maintenance, no tenants defaulting, no multiple tenants to manage
5. Only then will the sale and the close become frictionless
- Make sure the landlord has the utmost confidence in you

Notes: The author also says you can make a landlord become motivated, but he does not explain how in this talk.

#### Consolidate tenants into your best houses

Status: needs review

When rooms emptied during COVID, Shaun did not keep every property. He moved the remaining tenants from the weaker houses into the nice ones in great areas near the city centre, then handed the weaker houses and most Airbnbs back to landlords. You keep what has potential and cut the rest.

1. Sort your properties: the nice houses with potential, in a great area close to the city centre, versus the terrible ones you never spent much money on.
2. Move the remaining tenants out of the weak houses and into the nice ones.
3. Hand the weak houses back to the landlords.
4. Hand back most of the Airbnbs; keep the working-professional houses that run well.
- He also sourced a couple of his own properties on to other operators.

#### Cupboards and a fridge in every room

Five strangers sharing one kitchen leads to 6 a.m. calls about stolen butter. Put cupboards, a small fridge and, where you can, a sink in each room, so tenants keep their own food in their own space. It saves you a headache.

- Fit cupboards in each room as much as possible
- Give each tenant space for their own fridge in their room
- Where the layout allows, add a sink and a bit of kitchen space in the room
- Built-in wardrobes make things simpler and use the space well
- Reason: with five people sharing one kitchen you get early-morning calls about stolen food

#### Direct debit portal that flags failed payments

Also known as: Gold Cardless

Register each tenant on a direct debit portal so their top-up is collected automatically and you are told the moment something goes wrong. The author uses a service the transcript calls Gold Cardless. The most valuable alert is a cancelled direct debit, which lets you ring the tenant before rent is lost.

1. Register the tenant on the portal
2. The portal tells you three things: when a payment succeeds, when a payment fails, and when the tenant cancels the direct debit
3. On a cancellation, get straight on the phone to the tenant, find out why and mitigate any loss of rent before it happens

#### Direct to vendor marketing

Also known as: Direct-to-vendor marketing, Online ads for landlords

Go straight to property owners and tell them what you offer. Online, that means posting on social media, running ads and telling everyone what you do. Offline, it means printing leaflets and handing them out in local shops or paying someone to deliver them.

Online:
- Talk about what you do on social media, loudly and often.
- Run ads.
- Tell absolutely everyone what you do.

Offline, which Shaun says works just as well:
- Print leaflets.
- Hand them out in local shops.
- Or pay someone to go out and deliver leaflets for you.

### Also taught as: Online ads for landlords

- Keep the ad very simple
- Make the benefits very clear: features and benefits
- Remember you are solving the landlord's problem; showcase how well you solve it

#### Educate landlords new to social housing

Also known as: The educational side

Many landlords have never heard of temporary accommodation. Michael's third landlord was one of them. He explained the model before pitching, and the deal closed in two days at £1,000 a month profit.

- Find out whether the landlord knows what social housing and temporary accommodation are
- If not, make them aware of everything before you pitch the service
- Example: third property, found direct on OpenRent, landlord educated, closed in 2 days, £1,000 a month profit

#### Emergency room now, better-fit house on Monday

Also known as: Weekend placement then move

Status: needs review

When an assessment shows someone needs a roof immediately, Shaun's team does not wait for the ideal room. They give the person any available room in one of their houses over the weekend, then move them on the Monday to the house that suits them better. The person is safe tonight and well placed within days.

1. Read the assessment and the person's conduct. If they clearly need somewhere immediately, act on that.
2. Fit around practical limits: the team leader works Monday to Friday, and this tenant could only travel down from Warwickshire at the weekend.
3. Give a room straight away in whichever house has one, even if it is not the best match, so the person is housed over the weekend.
4. When back on Monday, move them to the house that is a better suited environment for them.
5. Tell the person up front that a better place may follow, so they take the first room without fear of being stuck: "let me know if you don't like that place, I think I might have somewhere better for you."

Notes: Told by Tyler, the housing team leader, with the tenant confirming.

#### Event pricing with a pricing platform

Also known as: Pricing labs

In serviced accommodation you do not set one flat price. A pricing platform reads the market and sets the right rate for each date, so you charge far more when a special event fills your area.

- Use a pricing platform. The author used one he calls pricing labs.
- It analyses the market and sets the right pricing structure for each specific date.
- In a prime location with a special event on, you can charge three or even four times the usual amount.

#### Extract pain points, then sell the solution

Also known as: Make your offer so good they feel stupid saying no, Pain points first, Sell to the pain point, Be part of the solution

Do not pitch first. Get the landlord to tell you about their experience in property and why social housing interests them now, so their problems are all on the table. Then present your service as the answer to exactly those problems. Sean borrows Alex Hormozi's line: make the offer so good they feel stupid saying no.

1. Ask about their pain points. Useful questions: What has been your experience in property? Why are you now interested in social housing? Why is this a viable option for you now?
2. Listen carefully and let them extract all their pain points. Sean pictures ten minutes of the landlord telling you all their problems.
3. Sell your service as the solution to those specific pain points.
4. Result: after laying out their problems and hearing your solution, they feel stupid saying no. Sean quotes the slogan of Alex Hormozi's book: make your offers so good that people feel stupid for saying no.

The skill is being able to effectively and competently extract that information.

Notes: Transcript spells the name 'Alex Hermosi'; the intended name is Alex Hormozi.

#### Find common ground

Also known as: Synergy

Rapport starts faster when you and the agent share something: a language, a background, a place. Shaun watches for it and uses it. When Sandu and an agent both spoke Romanian, they connected straight away and two properties were on the table within minutes.

- Look for common ground the moment you walk in: shared language, origin, area, anything real you both have.
- Use it to create synergy and connect before you talk business.
- Example: Sandu and the agent both spoke Romanian; the agent showed two three-bed properties at £1,500 and asked them to return in an hour when his brother, the lettings agent, was back.

#### Follow the people who do what you want to do

Also known as: Understand who you're following

Social media is useful if you use it for the right things. Shaun's rule is simple: check who you follow, and if you want to be a property entrepreneur, follow property entrepreneurs. Your feed becomes part of your environment and your source of advice.

1. Understand who you are currently following
2. Decide what you want to become
3. Follow people who already do that, for example property entrepreneurs if that is your goal
- Reason: social media is great if you use it for the correct things, and it lets you learn from people who have been through it

#### Get the inspector's check sheet in advance

Every council has a check sheet its inspector works from. Ask for it as soon as you start with a new council, go through it yourself and fix everything before the inspection. Otherwise you get a list of remedial works and lose two weeks.

1. When you start with a new council, ask for the specific check sheet the inspector will be using.
2. Go through the check sheet yourself before the inspector comes.
3. Make sure every item is boxed off.
4. Result: you are more likely to pass straight away and get the property onboarded as soon as the inspector comes out.

What happens if you skip it: the inspector comes out, gives you loads of remedial works and you waste a further two weeks. The author says it seems basic but many people forget it.

Sources:
- R-YT-y6ixpf6kK8Y (09:00)
- R-YT-y6ixpf6kK8Y (10:01)

#### Get the surveyor checklist up front

Ask the council for the surveyor's checklist before the inspection and walk the property ticking every item. It is the only way the author knows to pass first time and get paid without delay. Failing the survey wastes time while the landlord waits.

- Request the surveyor checklist before the survey happens
- Walk the property with paper and pen and tick the items off one by one
- Aim: pass straight away, onboard the property and get it into payout
- Why: the landlord or agent is waiting to close; you want to look competent and not waste time

- R-YT-E9HYGweF8Aw (07:01)

#### Get where the council and providers are

Also known as: Go to social housing events, Do whatever it takes

In social housing, councils and providers are too busy to build relationships with you by email. Shaun's answer is to go where they already are: social housing events, wherever in the country they happen. Travel, book a hotel and treat it as the price of survival.

1. Accept that councils and providers are busy and hard to reach the normal way.
2. Think outside the box: where can you meet these people and get to know them?
3. Go to social housing events and get where these people are.
4. Travel across the country if you have to. Book a hotel or an Airbnb.
5. Do whatever it takes; your business only survives if you are willing to.

Result for Shaun: contacts with people all over the country, which he says contributed to his success.

Notes: Given as a social housing example, but the same logic drives his whole relationship habit.

#### Give it at least five years

Also known as: Manage your own expectations, Expect the failures

Before he started, Shaun told himself the game would take at least five years to understand. Because he expected a long, bumpy road, every early failure felt like part of the plan instead of a reason to quit. Setting that expectation up front is what made it easy to get back up.

1. Before you get into the game, decide that it will take at least five years, and probably more.
2. Use those five years to understand the game, how it moves and the marketplace, to move correctly and to build your skillset.
3. When you fail and stumble at the start, tell yourself: this is what is meant to happen. Shaun failed a lot and calls it an emotional roller coaster.
4. Because you expected it, it becomes much easier to pick yourself back up and carry on.

- R-YT-eutIKciI3fo (07:01)

#### Grab the surveyor slot and remove the landlord's obstacles

Ask the council how long a surveyor takes and take the first slot they have, even a cancellation the next morning. Then call the landlord and make attending effortless. If they cannot be there, offer to collect the key, meet the surveyor and bring it back.

1. Ask the council directly: "What is the waiting time for a surveyor to come out?" Accept any cancellation.
2. Call the landlord straight away: "I've got some fantastic news. The council are saying the surveyor is able to come out tomorrow at 10:00 a.m. Would that work for you?"
3. If they hesitate: "If you can't come, don't worry. We'll come and meet you wherever you are, get the key off you, do the survey and bring the key back to you."
4. Confirm and move on; do not let the slot slip.

- R-YT-HJ5NOvSeCDA (54:02)

#### Half an hour of talking about anything

Also known as: Build rapport on a friendship level

Deals come from rapport, and rapport comes from talking about anything but business. Shaun will spend half an hour on a call with a council or landlord without touching business. That half hour saves a lot of time later, because people who like you offer you more property.

- Talk about absolutely anything; get them onto a friendship level.
- It works the same whatever you are doing and whoever you deal with.
- Shaun's habit: half an hour on a call with the council or a landlord about nothing to do with business.
- Payoff: that half hour makes you and saves you an awful lot of time; people become willing to offer you more and more properties.

#### HMO public register method

Also known as: HMO public register app, Finding HMO landlords through the council's public register, HMO public register leads, HMO licences are public, HMO, House of multiple occupants, Direct-to-vendor letters from the HMO register, Direct to vendor letters

Every council has an HMO department, and its website carries a public register of licensed HMOs. Licence holders must show their name and address, or their company's. That register is a ready made list of landlords you can contact with a guaranteed rent offer.

1. Note that councils and housing associations sometimes need HMOs for temporary accommodation.
2. Every council has an HMO department with a website.
3. On the website, find the HMO public register.
4. Once a landlord gets a licence, they have to display their name and address, or the company name and address if it is registered to a company.
5. Acquire this database of licence holders.
6. Market your services to these landlords, offering guaranteed rent.

Shaun has used this for 6 and a half years and says it dramatically helped grow his business.

Notes: The transcript renders it as 'HML public Register App'; from context it is the HMO public register that councils publish. The chunk does not expand HMO or describe the app beyond the council website.

#### Home emergency cover with the tenant as account holder

Take out a home emergency cover policy on the property and register the tenant as the account holder. When something breaks, the tenant calls the cover company, not you, and they send someone out. The author calls this a clever way to systemise the business.

1. Take out a home emergency cover service on the property
2. Put the tenant down as the account holder
3. If the tenant has an issue they contact the cover company directly
4. The cover company goes out and resolves it
- Result: fewer calls to you and a more systemised operation

Sources:
- R-YT-E9HYGweF8Aw (09:01)

#### Insure after the first deal

Also known as: No money down, then insurances

You do not need thousands in the bank before you start. Most insurances can be arranged after your first deal, so the order is: close a no money down deal, get the money in, then buy the cover. This is how students with no savings get going.

1. Structure the first deal so it needs no money down.
2. Get the money coming in as quickly as possible.
3. As soon as the money arrives, get all your insurances boxed off.
- Most insurances, though not all, can be taken out after the first deal.
- Many students do not have thousands ready, so this order is how the academy structures it.

Sources:
- R-YT-NuYOOjmh0ZI (19:01)
- R-YT-NuYOOjmh0ZI (20:01)

#### Introduce yourself to both neighbours

Every time you take on a property, knock on both neighbours' doors, introduce yourself and leave your name, number and work email. If a problem arises they call you, not the landlord or the council. Complaints that reach either of those make you look incompetent.

1. On taking a new property, visit the neighbours on either side in person; the author did this himself at first and now sends a housing manager
2. Give them your contact name, phone number and work email address
3. Any issue then comes to you
- Why: complaints to a rent-to-rent landlord or to the busy council make your business look incompetent
- You are the provider and responsible for your tenants, for the house being managed well and for the neighbourhood being happy

- R-YT-E9HYGweF8Aw (12:00)
- R-YT-E9HYGweF8Aw (13:00)

#### Keep incentives separate and roll them into the next property

New businesses struggle with cash up front. The student treats the incentive as a separate pot from monthly earnings. He uses some of it to pay bills when needed and rolls the rest into acquiring the next property.

1. Keep the incentive separate from your monthly earnings.
2. Use some of it to pay bills if you need to.
3. Roll the rest into another property, found through OpenRent, a similar site, or a sourcer.

Sources:
- R-YT-bqJS1k17GVo (19:01)

#### Keep the agent and landlord warm after a viewing

Also known as: Landlord pack, Staying credible, Get personal numbers to maintain the relationship, Losing the landlord while the council catches up, Keeping the landlord warm

Building a relationship is half of sales; the other half is keeping it alive. Other people want the same property, so the viewing is not the finish line. Get the person's personal number, send a thank-you and a landlord pack, and stay in front of them every day until the deal is done. Being credible and memorable is what keeps you in first place.

1. Be very personal in the conversation itself; that is how rapport forms.
2. Always get the personal number (personal WhatsApp), not just the office line. Shaun says he maintains every relationship this way.
3. Straight after the viewing, send a thank-you message saying the property is perfect for you.
4. Tell them you will send a landlord pack when you leave. It makes you more memorable than everyone else and gives them something to show their partner.
5. Message every single day with an update, repeating that you definitely want the property and would love to do business with them.
6. Maintain the relationship until keys and contract are in hand. Maintain it well and you get lots of deals.

#### Key retractor on the lock box

Also known as: Key retractor

A key retractor is a small device that screws into your existing key lock box so the key retracts instead of coming away. It stops contractors, cleaners and staff walking off with the key in their pocket and losing it. Shaun calls it a game changer and now fits one at every property.

The problem it fixes:
- Contractors, cleaners and even staff members take the key out of the lock box, it goes in their pocket, and it gets lost. Shaun calls this one of the biggest issues in properties and a nightmare.

The fix:
- Fit a key retractor. It screws into the existing lock box, and the key retracts rather than being carried away.
- Shaun now does this at all of his properties.

Sources:
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (01:00)
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (02:00)

#### Knock on the door next door

Also known as: Neighbouring landlord approach

Status: needs review

When you check tenants into a property, look at the neighbours. Michael saw a landlord refurbishing two doors down, knocked, introduced himself and offered to help. They met two hours later and he closed the property a week later with no money down.

1. Notice landlords nearby, for example one refurbishing a property or one who approaches your tenants
2. Knock on the door and introduce yourself with something like: "Look, I've just taken on the property two doors down. I would love to have a conversation with you to see how I can be of assistance to you"
3. Meet quickly; Michael met this landlord 2 hours later
4. Show the property you already run and the condition you keep it in
- Result: closed a week later, no deposit, no money down
- This landlord had been let down by another social housing company
- Michael also only works with landlords he likes

Sources:
- R-YT-VPA30_DJvZs (24:02)
- R-YT-VPA30_DJvZs (25:00)

#### Landline and call answering service from day one

Also known as: Soho 66, Start as you mean to go on, Call answering service, Someone always answers the phone

Set up a business landline and a call answering service before your first lead calls. Someone always picks up, the call is recorded, and you get the details to call back when you are free. It makes a one-person business look like a team, which reassures providers and landlords.

1. From day one, get a landline number for the business.
2. Sign up to a call answering service. Sean uses Soho 66 and has for years; there are other companies.
3. The service answers calls on your behalf, records every call and collects the key information.
4. They send you a voice recording of the conversation so you can call the lead back when you are ready.

Why it matters:
- Most people starting out still have a job and cannot answer the phone all day
- Once you market, people will call; if nobody answers, you look unprofessional
- Even a small company looks like it has several people, which subconsciously builds confidence with providers and landlords
- Start as you mean to go on: set the back end up properly at the start

How fast you respond to queries, and how professional you look while doing it, makes a big difference to whether deals close.

### Also taught as: Someone always answers the phone

- Unanswered calls make a provider or landlord think the worst about how you will respond to future issues
- Make sure someone is there whenever the phone rings
- Options: a full-time VA; if the VA is busy, a call answering service

#### Landlord lease: 3 to 5 years with a one-sided break clause

Also known as: Lease length and break clause

When you take a landlord's property, you agree how long you keep it and who can walk away early. The author takes properties for three to five years with a break clause of three to six months that only he can use. The risk sits with you, so the security should too.

- Lease term with the landlord: usually 3 to 5 years.
- Break clause: typically 3 to 6 months.
- The break clause is for you only, not the landlord, because the risk is all on you.
- Why: you may have to invest in the property, and the provider or council wants security. You do not want a tenant placed and then the landlord asking for the property back after three months.
- Most landlords accept this because what they want is a long-term profitable solution.

#### Lead with council backed and government funded

Also known as: Closing landlords and agents

When you pitch a landlord or an agent, put the council at the front of your offer. The rent is council backed and government funded, which gives them confidence in you. Many landlords got stung in Covid, so also point out that this strategy is recession proof.

- When trying to close a landlord or an agent, lead with the fact that this is a council backed, government funded strategy.
- Effect: it gives the agent or landlord the utmost confidence in you and your strategy.
- Add that the strategy is recession proof. Many landlords, Shaun included, got stung in Covid because of the nationwide crisis, so this lands.
- Combine with the guaranteed rent message: the rent is paid directly by the council, so it really is guaranteed.

#### Learn the systems inside someone else's business first

Shaun wanted his own car business but did not know the systems or processes. So he went to work in a car dealership for a few years to learn how it works and build his leadership skills, planning to start his own later. He calls joining Ford the best decision he made.

1. Decide the business you want to own.
2. Admit what you do not understand yet. For Shaun it was the systems and processes.
3. Go and work inside an established company in that industry for a few years.
4. Learn exactly how it works, the systems and all the processes, and build your leadership skills.
5. Then go and start your own.

Shaun left his father's business in 2014 to work at Ford with this plan.

Also known as: Keeping the council warm

#### Nail your compliance and setup

Know the council's exact requirements, then have the people and suppliers lined up to meet them. That means reliable tradesmen for remedial work and furniture suppliers who deliver on time, because the furnishing rules change from council to council.

1. Understand exactly what the council requires
2. Have competent and reliable tradesmen in place to do the job and any remedial work
3. Plan the furnishing: you will need to furnish the property
   - Some councils require a full set of bedroom furniture

1. In the negotiation, ask the landlord to include the refurb and the white goods in the package
2. Reason: once you have the property you can start straight away and get tenants in as quickly as possible
3. Your side of the bargain: look after the property and keep everything in working order

#### Negotiating a rent-free period with landlords

Also known as: Three months rent free

During COVID, landlords had empty properties they could not fill. Shaun offered to take them on with a rent-free period at the start, in return for long-term guaranteed rent. He could prove the market was dead because his own rooms were not filling even at six weeks rent free. His first two deals came with three months rent free.

1. Understand the landlord's position: the property is empty and, in lockdown, they are not going to fill it.
2. Make the offer: I can take it off you, but I am going to need some time, and it will be worth your while because you get long-term guaranteed rent.
3. Back it with proof from your own portfolio. Shaun showed his own properties on six weeks rent free with no enquiries and said: this is what I do, I am management, so you have no chance of filling it.
4. Ask for the period you need. He asked for three months because nobody knew how long COVID would last.
5. Use the rent-free months to get tenants in and start making profit before rent is due.
- Result: three months rent free on his first two deals. He calls this his breakthrough, and being creative with deal structure is what let him scale.

Notes: The argument worked because it was true at the time: landlords could not argue with it. It depends on a market where the landlord genuinely cannot fill the property.

#### Negotiating away the deposit

Also known as: No deposit, Work backwards from the deposit, Deposit response: this is not a normal tenancy, Why we do not pay a deposit

Landlords ask for a deposit to cover unpaid rent or damage. Work backwards from those two fears and remove them. Show your finances, show your inspection process, and explain why a company tenant is a lower risk than a normal one.

1. Understand why a landlord wants a deposit: in case you default on rent or mess up the property
2. Work backwards: how do you make the landlord comfortable that neither will happen
3. Show your financial position
4. Show the processes that keep the property up to standard: a quarterly inspection on every property, either a physical inspection the landlord can attend or an inventory report they sign off
5. Show, do not just tell; proof is everything with someone you are meeting for the first time
6. Remind them you are not a normal tenant: you maintain the property and you guarantee the rent
7. Explain that as a company that does not live there, you do not have the same tenant rights, so you are easier to remove than a tenant
- Watch for the reflex: most landlords have never let this way, treat you as a normal tenant and are used to drilling for a deposit
- Shaun has paid a deposit on about three of all his properties; Michael on none

#### Negotiating the rate with the council

Also known as: Council lowballing

The council is a business and will lowball you to save money. Know the value of the service you are offering and hold your ground. Make sure enough is left in the budget to cover the landlord's maintenance, because that falls on you.

- Expect a low first offer; the council is trying to save the most money it can
- Know the service you provide and be clear about it
- Keep both sides in mind: the council and the landlord
- Full maintenance of the property usually sits with your company, so the rate must leave enough budget to fix anything that goes wrong

In temporary accommodation you house one family per property, so you can put one adult from that household on the maintenance account. When something breaks, they ring the maintenance company directly instead of you. You pay for the service, but the calls stop landing on your phone.

1. A temporary accommodation property holds one family, so the household stays the same. Pick one adult from it.
2. Put that person on the account with your maintenance company for that property.
3. Give them the company's number. For a gas, boiler, electrics, leaky tap or leak problem they call the maintenance company directly, not you.
4. If they need extra assistance, they can call your VA, who is on hand.

#### Outsource viewings and inspections to a viewing company

Also known as: Vubet, Vueer, Viewer, VBEC, Third-party property viewings

You do not have to drive two or three hours to look at a property. Shaun uses a company he calls Vubet, one of his game changers, to view properties, attend inspections, supervise maintenance, collect keys and inspect tenanted homes on his behalf. It is cheap and it lets you work in areas far from home.

What the company can do for you, in the order a deal runs:
1. Property viewings, at a couple of hours' notice. You get a full detailed breakdown: a video, comments on the property and its condition, and lots of pictures, so you have a full picture without travelling. Cost: around 35 to 40 per viewing.
2. Attend the council surveyor's inspection on your behalf if you cannot be there.
3. Help facilitate the maintenance from the remedial report: be on site to make sure the job gets done properly and inspect the work before you book the surveyor back out. Shaun calls this an absolute game changer.
4. Collect the keys from the estate agent or the landlord on your behalf.
5. Do your fortnightly tenant inspections once the tenant has moved in.

Shaun's view: it is cheap, it is affordable and it is a great way of leveraging, especially when the property is two to three hours away. He still recommends attending the council inspection yourself when you can, because that is where relationships are built.

Agents often bristle when you approach them because they think you want their business. Show them the opposite: their agreement with the landlord stays as it is, they keep their 10 to 15 percent, and you do the tenant-finding and management. Same pay, much less work.

1. Reassure the agent you are not taking their business or ruining any existing agreement or contract with their landlord
2. Explain the arrangement: you manage the property and find the tenants; they still get paid their 10 to 15% and pass the rent on to the landlord

#### Post-it notes on every remedial job

With a dozen small jobs across a house it is easy to forget one. Walk round with Post-it notes and stick one wherever work is needed. Peel each off when the job is done, so you can see progress and nothing gets missed.

1. After the survey, walk the property with a pad of Post-it notes.
2. Stick a note on every item the surveyor flagged: doors, windows, walls, boxing-in.

When a council expects you to cover the bills, do not just absorb the cost. Put the bills into your offer and ask for more. Councils usually pay the extra because they want to avoid the admin, not because they are short of money.

#### Protecting the property from tenant damage

Under the nightly rate model, damage caused by the tenant comes out of your pocket. The author's two defences are a good relationship with the tenant and regular inspections. A good relationship helps, but never replace inspections with the tenant's word.

2. Do not take the tenant's word for it; do regular inspections

#### Quarterly property inspections

Also known as: Inspections, Inventory report

Michael inspects every property he takes on, every quarter. He looks for damp and mould, and he goes mainly because tenants who do not want to be a bother never report faults. The report also reassures the landlord that their property is in good hands.

- Inspect each property every quarter
- Look for problems such as damp and mould
- The point is not to catch tenants damaging the property. Tenants often stay quiet about faults because they do not want to bother you, so the visit tells them you are happy to jump on any problem
- Spot what could be going wrong and get someone out immediately to fix it
- You can hire an inventory company to do the inspection for you and produce a full, detailed report, so you do not have to visit
- Give that report to every landlord; the landlord can also join a physical inspection or sign off the inventory report

#### Receivers instead of thermostats

Also known as: Heating receiver with timer, Timer receiver

In shared houses where you pay the gas and electric, tenants with a thermostat tend to leave the heating on all day and night. Shaun swaps the thermostat for a receiver: the tenant presses a timer for 30 minutes, 1 hour or 2 hours, and the heating cuts off after that. Nobody has to remember to turn it off, so the boiler is not running while the house is empty or asleep.

The problem:
- When tenants have access to the thermostat, many leave the heating on 24/7. Shaun walked into a house that felt like a sauna because the boiler temperature was set very high.
- The business pays the gas and electric, not the tenant, so this hits the profitability of that household directly.

The fix:
1. Replace the thermostat with a receiver.
2. The tenant has to manually put the timer on: 30 minutes, 1 hour or 2 hours.
3. After the chosen time the heating cuts off.

Why it works: at night, and in the day when tenants are out, the boiler is not left on unnecessarily. Shaun calls it a game changer.

On inspection: if a house is very hot, check whether it has a receiver fitted.

Sources:
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (02:00)
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (03:01)

When you call a provider, tell them you found them through the contract they just won. It shows you did your homework, gives you instant common ground, and makes you stand out from everyone else cold calling them.

1. Before you call, read the contract page on Contracts Finder: what it is for, the framework, the end date.
2. Open the conversation by referencing how you found them and which contract it was.
3. Use that shared ground to ask whether they are looking to scale under that contract.

Why: it creates common ground straight away and makes you stand out.

#### Secure the property with a holding deposit

Also known as: Week's rent holding deposit

A landlord on OpenRent has other viewings booked, so ask what it takes to secure the house today. Offer what a normal tenant would offer, a week's rent as a holding deposit, and ask for the listing to come down. Get the terms written into the contract.

1. Ask the landlord straight out: "What can we do to secure the property today?"
2. Offer a week's rent as a holding deposit, the same as a tenant would pay through OpenRent.
3. Ask for the bank details and send the money the same day.
4. Ask the landlord to take the property off OpenRent as soon as the payment lands.
5. Put the arrangement in the contract: the holding deposit is deducted from the first month's rent.
6. With that done, collect the keys.

#### Send the lease for digital signature, then call

Do not post a contract and wait. Email the commercial lease for digital signing to both parties, use software that tells you when the landlord opens it, and phone them to say it is on its way and you are happy to explain anything.

1. Prepare the commercial lease with the agreed terms.
2. Send it to your own email and the landlord's for digital signing.
3. Watch the open notification from the signing software so you know when to expect questions.
4. Call the landlord: "I'm just letting you know we've sent across the contract. If there's anything you don't understand, just give me a call. More than happy to assist."
5. Once signed, you are good to go.

#### Shadow first, then take the lead

Shaun's coaching method over the two days: on day one he does the agent visits while the learner watches; on day two the learner leads and he watches, steps in only if they struggle, and debriefs on what could have been better. You learn this by doing it, not by hearing it.

1. Day one: the coach does a few visits so the learner can see how it is done.
2. Day two: the learner takes the lead at every agent. The coach is there to watch, coach and advise.
3. The coach steps in only if the agent asks lots of questions and the learner is visibly struggling.
4. After each visit: show exactly where it went wrong and what could have been done better.
5. Reason: that is how you learn, by actually doing it.

Notes: The learner's own reflection after day one: knowledge in the game gives you the confidence.

#### Shadow the best and copy them

When Shaun started at Ford with no experience, he picked the top salesman who sat in front of him, watched what worked and copied it. Within two months he was top of the leaderboard. He still does this in business and life: find who is doing well, pick the traits you like, copy them.

1. Identify the person who is performing best around you. At Ford it was the top salesman sitting opposite.
2. Shadow them: watch what they do and what is working well for them.
3. Copy it, as simply as that.
4. In business more broadly: look at who is doing well, ask which character traits you like in that person, and copy those.

Result for Shaun: second on the leaderboard in his second month, then first, with no previous motor trade knowledge or skills.

#### Start small and increase the intensity

Also known as: Build the habit first

To make a new habit stick, set the bar low enough that it is easy, then raise it once the habit is there. The speaker's ten pages a day is the worked example: small, easy, daily, and it grows on its own. The habit comes first, the intensity later.

1. Pick a small daily minimum, small enough that it is nice and easy. The speaker uses 10 pages of a book.
2. Do it daily to build the habit.
3. Progressively increase the intensity once the habit is there. In practice you often exceed the minimum on your own, for example reading 20 pages instead of 10.
4. Keep the minimum as the floor you never go under.

Also known as: Rent-free period

- Aim for the start date to match the tenants moving in

#### The property inspection day

Also known as: Being on the ground, Property visits, Property inspection three: the new build

When Shaun comes back to the UK he spends a day on the ground: he catches up with his team leader, then walks a series of houses and talks to the tenants. He checks how the houses are maintained, spots problems and hears how the service feels from inside. It is how an owner who is not there every day keeps a true picture of the business.

How the day runs:
1. Catch up with the housing team leader: how the business is performing, his views, his concerns.
2. Visit properties in the team's patches and look for any concerns.
3. Speak to tenants in each house: how they find the service, the staff and the house, and whether there are any issues.
4. Where something is wrong, deal with it on the spot. Examples from the day: a house that is too hot, so check for a heating receiver; a smell of weed from a room; a damp repair in progress.
5. Close the day with a verdict on maintenance, tenant feedback and the team.

What Shaun looked at: how well each house is maintained, communal spaces, tenants' rooms, works in progress, and who has been moved in.

#### Visit every estate agent in the town

#### Visualize success

Also known as: Visualisation, Tricking your mind into success

Picture the outcome you want every day, in detail. Shaun says the mind runs the same process for a vivid visualisation as for reality, so it treats the goal as already real and starts finding ways to get there. It also conditions you to notice opportunities you would otherwise walk past.

- Practice: visualise your success every single day. Shaun visualised the day he would hand in his notice at work, every day, and thought about it all the time.
- Why he says it works: your mind has the same identical process in visualisation and in actual reality. It does not know the difference, so it thinks the goal is already reality. You are, in effect, tricking your mind into success.
- If you can see it in your mind, you can create the reality. Once you visualise it, you find a way of making it happen, and it becomes a self-fulfilling prophecy.
- Side effect: it conditions your mind for success and to spot opportunities (see the Porsche example).
- He acknowledges it can sound silly to someone who has never done it, and insists on it anyway.

#### Working out the profit on a council property

Also known as: Deal maths, Nightly rate times 30, Worked example: pay the landlord £1,500, keep £1,000, Deal arithmetic

Before you chase a property you need one number: what the council pays per month minus what the landlord wants. Shaun does this in seconds on the phone and it decides which listings are worth a call. It also tells you how many properties you need for the income you want.

1. Take the council's nightly rate and multiply by 30. Three bed in Luton: £65 x 30 = £1,950 a month.
2. Search OpenRent and the agents for three and four beds around your target rent.
3. Profit = council income minus the rent you pay the landlord. £1,950 - £1,500 = £450 a month. A £1,450 property gives about £500.
4. Check who pays the bills first. In Luton the tenant pays utilities and the landlord does repairs, so the margin holds.
5. Set a ceiling and say it to agents: max £1,500 for a three bed.
6. Walk away from thin deals. A four bed at £1,600 gave only £70 profit.
7. Think in scale: £450 on ten properties, and the council needs 300.

#### Does this business align with my purpose?

Also known as: Purpose check, What's written for them may not be written for you

Before you start a business, check that it fits you. Hardship will come, and by then the early motivation will be gone; discipline only holds if you are acting within your purpose. Shaun does not copy what others do well, because what is written for them may not be written for you.

Ask yourself, honestly:
1. Does this business align with my purpose?
2. What do I like doing?
3. What are my strengths?
4. What are my goals?
5. What is my purpose, more importantly, and does the business align with it?
6. When I hit hardship, will I have the discipline and the resilience to work through it and come out the other end?
- Why: challenges and obstacles will come, and the motivation you started with will have faded by then. Discipline is very hard to build if you are not acting within your purpose.
- Do not follow people just because they do something well and effortlessly. It is written for them, and it may not be written for you.

#### Four ways into social housing

Also known as: The three strategies we recommend, Rent-to-rent side and buying side, BRR social housing

Social housing and temporary accommodation sit under one umbrella, and there are four ways in. Becoming a provider yourself is hard and full of red tape. The hosts recommend and teach the other three: buy and lease to a provider, rent-to-rent with a back-to-back lease to a provider, or rent-to-rent temporary accommodation with the council.

1. Become a social housing provider yourself: a lot of red tape, very hard to do.
2. Buy a property and lease it to a social housing provider.
3. Rent-to-rent with a back-to-back lease to an existing provider: no involvement in the care or support side.
4. Rent-to-rent temporary accommodation: work with the council directly.
- Options 2 to 4 are the ones they recommend and teach, to keep it simple for beginners who find social housing confusing.

#### Rules for raising finance from investors

Also known as: Tips for working with investors

Tips from the hosts for anyone borrowing from or partnering with private investors. Pick people you genuinely like for long-term joint ventures, never borrow more than you can handle, and give yourself a safety buffer on the loan term so a slow project does not turn into a sleepless crisis.

- Long-term JV: make sure you actually like the person and get along, because you will be tied together for years.
- Do not overstretch on how much you borrow.
- Get the term right. If the project will take 12 or 24 months, do not sign a 6 or 12 month agreement. Having the money feels great on day one, but if the project is not finished when the money is due you are under immense stress.
- Build in a safety buffer: sign a longer term than you expect to need.
- Under-promise, over-deliver: sign 18 months, finish in 12, and tell the investor the good news that you can pay them back six months early. That builds your reputation, instead of approaching month 12 unable to sleep.

#### The four tenant types

Also known as: Who goes into temporary accommodation

Four groups of people end up in temporary and emergency accommodation. Knowing them tells you what kind of demand a council has and what kind of property it needs. Shaun lists the homeless, domestic violence survivors, people hit by emergencies, and refugees and asylum seekers.

1. The homeless. Numbers are rising because of the cost of living, evictions and high energy prices. People who cannot maintain their home get evicted, the council has to house them, and they go into temporary and emergency accommodation.
2. Domestic violence. Depending on the severity, the person needs a safe house, sometimes at the opposite end of the country. They also go into temporary and emergency accommodation.
3. Emergency situations such as fire and floods. The tenants need to be placed in temporary and emergency accommodation.
4. Refugee and asylum housing needs. When people are granted access into the country they are first housed in temporary accommodation until they get leave to remain, then they move to permanent accommodation.

#### Three core strategies for 2026

Also known as: The three core social housing strategies, Three main ways within rent-to-rent, Three core strategies, The three strategies taught on the programme

The author's shortlist of social housing strategies that work best in 2026. Each has a different payer, risk level and workload. He recommends you choose one.

1. Temporary accommodation on a nightly rate: council-paid, long-term demand, higher income and higher responsibility; you manage the property and the tenant
2. The A model: single occupants in an HMO, paid directly by DWP at around the LHA rate plus a tenant top-up; incentives up front; more tenants to manage and more risk, but tends to make more money
3. Back-to-back leases: you sit between a landlord and a provider and keep the rent difference; virtually no risk, zero operations, easy no-money-down deals; the one he would start with from scratch

#### Three things social housing offers a landlord

Also known as: Long-term contracts, guaranteed rent, zero hassle, What the landlord gets: zero void, zero maintenance, zero hassle, Landlord benefits, Zero void, zero maintenance, zero hassle, The hassles a company let removes for the landlord, Company let benefits

Because social housing is paid directly by the government, Sean can promise a landlord three things private tenants cannot: a long-term contract, genuinely guaranteed rent and a zero-hassle, fully managed property. Cover all three and there is very little reason left to say no.

1. Long-term contracts. Possible because you are paid directly by the government.
2. Genuinely guaranteed rent. Same reason.
3. Zero hassle. You manage the whole property, so if the landlord has confidence in you they will believe it will be hassle-free.

These map onto what landlords want: profitable, secure, long-term, no headaches. Cover all three and you leave very little reason to refuse the offer.

### Also taught as: What the landlord gets: zero void, zero maintenance, zero hassle

What the landlord gets when they lease to you:
- Zero voids: the rent is guaranteed, so no empty months.
- Zero maintenance: you cover it.
- Zero hassle and zero headache from running the property.
- Long-term security: a multi-year commercial lease instead of a periodic tenancy.
You take all the hassle away from the landlord; that is the trade for the control you get.

#### Three tips for aspiring entrepreneurs

Also known as: Shaun's top tips

Asked for his top tips for anyone on the journey, Shaun gives three. Choose the people around you and take advice from those who have done it. Do not compare your start to someone else's twentieth step on social media. Work on yourself first, because the business cannot outgrow you.

1. Surround yourself with the people you want to be like. If someone is where you want to be, speak to that person. Do not take business advice from loved ones who have no successful business; find someone who has done it, ask how they started and get them to mentor you. Use social media and the networking events in your niche to find that circle.
2. Treat social media as a blessing and a curse. Do not compare your step one to someone else's step twenty, because you get discouraged and some of it is not even real. Be authentic, be who you are and go for it.
3. Work on yourself first. Your business cannot outgrow who you are, so always put yourself first. He only started to achieve success when he did, and then everything else happened organically.

#### Two reasons landlords are stressing right now

Also known as: Landlord pain points from the Renters Rights Bill

Status: needs review

These are the two problems the Renters Rights Bill created for landlords, and the pain points your offer should answer. First, tenancies are now periodic, so a landlord cannot lock in a long-term let and faces repeated voids and fees. Second, a landlord can no longer evict a tenant unless the tenant breaches the contract.

What a landlord wants: a long-term, profitable solution from their asset. The Bill removes that power.

1. Periodic tenancies remove the long-term solution.
   - No more 12-month agreements. That is a thing of the past.
   - Constant voids and constantly having to find new tenants.
   - When they let through an estate agent, the agent usually charges a tenant finding fee, so extra costs every couple of months if turnover is high.
   - Worst on HMOs: the market is flooded and saturated with HMOs, so tenants have lots of options and choice.
2. They can no longer evict unless the tenant is in breach of contract, usually arrears. Shaun calls this the huge one.
   - The landlord is stuck with the tenant until the tenant decides to leave.
   - They cannot convert the property, change its use, turn a family home into an HMO or a care home, or move into social housing when their circumstances change.

Sources:
- R-YT-JygqeccwlQU (05:00)
- R-YT-JygqeccwlQU (06:00)

1. Bookkeeping and accounts. Get a bookkeeper or accountant straight away rather than trying to do the books yourself.
2. Admin and lead generation. Hand phone calls, emails, research and chasing landlords and agents to a virtual assistant so your own time goes on high-value tasks.

#### Two ways to create value with a simple product

Also known as: Marketing plus bundling, Double the price

You can make the same simple product sell for more in two ways. First, make its marketing look better so people perceive it as worth more. Second, add another product so the customer gets more real value. Together they can double the price.

Starting point: a cup of lemonade at a hypothetical £2.
1. Look at the marketing. Raise the value perception by making your stand better, more colourful and more eye-catching so it stands out. People who walk past and see an appealing stand accept a higher price.
2. Create more value by adding an additional product. Divide sweets into bags of 10 and sell the lemonade plus a bag of 10 sweets. Now you can charge £4.

Result: the price has doubled, through value perception (way 1) and value creation (way 2).

- R-YT-OvrW406xkGs (05:01)

#### What the council surveyor asks for

Also known as: Remedial works, Council requirements

Status: needs review

This is the remedial list a council surveyor gave on a nearly new house. Most of it is safety and finish. Use it to check a property before you offer on it, so the works are light.

Safety:
- Window restrictors on every floor above ground level; an unrestricted upstairs window is a major safety risk
- Door stops on every door, so doors do not swing and break the wall
- Fire blanket in the kitchen
- Lock on the bathroom door
- All doors catching and closing properly; a door that does not close will be picked up straight away

Finish:
- Windows dressed with blinds or curtains in every room
- Kitchen pipework and gaps boxed in
- Missing screws in hinges replaced
- Unpainted doors and cupboard doors painted; patched walls sanded and repainted
- No signs of damp; the council is 'all over' damp

Furnishing:
- White goods: fridge, freezer, washing machine, dishwasher (this landlord had already provided them)
- Beds only; this council did not require wardrobes
- Carpet with underlay was a plus

Notes: Requirements come from one council on one property; other councils may differ. The house had no gas supply.

#### Why get into social housing

Also known as: Social housing gives real cash flow, Airbnb gives projections

People often ask Sean why he chose social housing over other property strategies. His answer comes down to five reasons, all about stability and speed. Together they explain why he calls it a strategy for all seasons.

- Recession proof: it works in all seasons, unlike Airbnb or professional lets, which failed him when COVID hit and tenants lost jobs
- Zero tenant marketing: the tenant is there up front, so you can scale with confidence
- Government-backed: financial stability for you and a selling point for landlords
- Premium rents: the UK housing crisis means councils are desperate and pay a premium, giving better monthly cash flow
- Upfront incentives: in some council deals you are paid at the start, which funds the next deal

**Scripts**

#### Answering a landlord burned by social housing

Also known as: The past won't matter script

What Shaun says to a landlord or agent who has had a bad social housing experience. He acknowledges the past, separates himself from it, explains how his company is different, and promises what every landlord wants: a profitable, long-term, zero-stress lease.

Say, in this order:
1. Acknowledge: "Look, I know you've had this issue in the past. I know you've had this experience."
2. Separate yourself: "The key difference is I wasn't a part of that deal. It had nothing to do with me."
3. Present yourself: "This is me. This is my company. This is how we operate. This is how we're different."
4. Protect their asset: "This is how I'm going to be able to protect your asset."
5. Promise the outcome: "You're going to have a nice, profitable, long-term solution. You're going to have a long-term lease with a long-term tenancy. It's going to be zero stress."

Sources:
- R-YT-fUN_l7UYES4 (11:00)

#### Ask the council what happens if the tenant defers

Before taking on A model tenants, ask the council openly what support you get as a provider if a tenant stops paying their top-up. The author says never be afraid to ask. It sets expectations and protects your income.

Say to the council: "We're more than happy to get involved in this strategy. But if the tenant defers, what help and support is there for me as the provider to get paid?"

Sources:
- R-YT-E9HYGweF8Aw (16:01)

#### Confronting a tenant about the smell of weed

How Shaun handled a room that smelt of cannabis during an inspection. He asks to speak to the tenant, names what he can smell, hears the denial, restates the rule without arguing, and closes with a clear instruction and a follow-up inspection. It is short, calm and leaves no doubt about what happens next.

1. Ask to speak to the tenant: "Can I see you, please?"
2. Name the problem directly: "I can smell weed coming from your room."
3. When the tenant says he smoked outside, describe what you noticed: "It smells really strong coming from your room." and "When you opened the door, it just hit me." Point out that everyone who walked into the house noticed the same thing.
4. Restate the rule: "You can't smoke anywhere on the premises. You can smoke cigarettes, but not weed. There's a park across the road. If you want to do it, just go and do it across the road."
5. Close with the instruction: "Best thing to do then moving forward is just don't. Don't do it at all on the premises."
6. Set the follow-up: "I'm going to come back tomorrow and do the inspection."

Sources:
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (18:01)
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (19:00)

#### Council discovery call

This is how Shaun opens with a council and what he asks before he hangs up. You introduce yourself as a provider with access to properties, then ask the questions that tell you whether the numbers work. Luton Council said yes to taking on more within one call.

Before you dial: make sure you are through to the temporary accommodation department, not a general line.

Opening:
"Hi, it's Sean. We're a temporary accommodation provider and we've got access to quite a lot of properties in the local area. We're looking at working with the council to provide temporary accommodation. Is this something that you guys are still offering at the moment?"

Then establish exactly what they need. Write the answers down:
1. What size properties do you need? (Luton: three and four beds.)
2. Where specifically? (Anywhere in Luton.)
3. How does the process work, in terms of working with you and price?
4. What are your nightly rates at the moment?
5. Are the rates negotiable?
6. What is your current demand? Do you have a lot of tenants on the waiting list?
7. Who covers the bills, the tenant or us? Who does repairs?

Close:
"Leave it with me. We're going to have to crunch some numbers now, speak to our network, and see what we can get and take it from there."

- R-YT-HJ5NOvSeCDA (09:00)
- R-YT-HJ5NOvSeCDA (10:00)

#### Estate agent walk-in pitch

Also known as: The estate agent pitch

This is what Shaun says when he walks into a lettings agent cold. It is short, plain and improvised around rapport: who you are, that you work with the council, what you need, and whether they do company lets. Sandu's simpler version on day two got two properties lined up.

Structure, kept loose and friendly:
1. "Hi, lettings please." Shake hands, first names.
2. Who you are, in one line: "We do temporary accommodation with the council" or "We work with Luton Council" or, for a newcomer, "We're property investors, new in the area."
3. What you need: "We're looking for three or four bedroom properties for families. Do you have anything on the books at all?" Timing: as soon as possible.
4. If price comes up: "We're looking at paying max £1,500 for a three-bedroom property."
5. Ask: "Do you guys do company lets as well?"
6. If the landlord's interest comes up: "We'll take it off you on a long-term let. Landlords like it because it's guaranteed rent. We pay the market rent."
7. Ask for a viewing today.

All sales should be improvisation: go in, build rapport, keep it simple, explain what you do and what you want.

Sandu's day-two version: "We are property investors, new in the area, and we're looking for three to four bed houses. As soon as possible." And to a sales desk: "We've been tasked by the council to look for three and four bed properties. Do you have any available right now?"

#### Live call to Luton Council for temporary accommodation

This is the recorded call Sean made during his documentary, approaching Luton Council as a temporary accommodation provider. It shows the exact questions to ask: what you need, where, how the process works, what you pay, and how big the demand is. Copy the order and the tone.

Opening:
"Hi, it's Sean Corn. We're a temporary accommodation provider and we've got access to quite a lot of properties in the local area in Luton. We're looking at working with Luton Council to provide temporary accommodation. Is this something that you guys are still offering at the moment?"

When they ask what size properties you have, turn it back to their need:
"Ideally, what I need to try and establish is exactly what you need. We can get access to one, two and three-bedroom properties."

Write down their answer and pin the location:
"Three and four beds. All right. Cool. Two seconds. Let me write this down. Where specifically?"

Ask how the process works:
"Can you explain how the process works in terms of working with you?"

Ask about money:
"How does it work in terms of the payment, just so we know what we're working with? What's your nightly rates at the moment?"
"Are these rates negotiable at all?"

Ask about demand:
"Just out of interest, what is your current demand? Do you have a lot of tenants on the waiting list?"

Close without committing:
"It's good for us to hear, because we are looking to expand in the area. Leave it with me. We're going to have to crunch some numbers now, speak to our network, and see what we can get and take it from there."

Notes: Sean introduces the clip as calling a provider, but the person on the line is speaking for Luton Council (agent and landlord approved contractor's list, property negotiators). The transcript renders his name as 'Sean Corn'. Filler words have been removed from the script; the citations keep them.

- R-YT-aRcmGZIV7Y4 (09:01)
- R-YT-aRcmGZIV7Y4 (10:01)

#### Post-viewing thank-you message

Send this to the landlord as soon as you leave the viewing. It thanks them, states clearly that you want the property, and opens the door for daily follow-up.

"Hey Dave, thank you for your time. Really appreciate you showing us the property. It's perfect for us. We definitely want the property, it's perfect for what we need it for, and we'd love the opportunity to do business with you."

Follow it with: "When we leave, I'll send you a pack." Then keep in touch every day.

#### Provider cold call and voicemail

When you call a temporary accommodation provider from Contracts Finder, you usually reach voicemail. This is the short message Shaun leaves, and the live version he uses when someone picks up. Say who you are, what you can supply, and make it easy to call back.

Voicemail version:
"Hi, my name is Sean. I'm a property investor local in the area and just wanted to see what you currently need housing wise as we may be able to work together and supply you with properties. Give me a call back on this number. I'm free all day today. I'm in the area all week. Love the opportunity to have a call with you. Thank you."

Live version:
"We're property investors in the local area and we're looking to work with temporary and emergency accommodation providers. Just wanted to find out if you're currently looking for more properties. We may be able to help you."

After the call:
1. Send them an email straight away so they have two ways back to you.
2. If they are not taking on now, ask for the right person's email and when they will be taking on again.
3. Call a few in an area, then move on; do not get stuck on numbers that are out of service.

#### Replying to a provider's email enquiry

A provider from Contracts Finder replied asking how you heard of them and which councils you work with. Shaun's answer gives just enough, avoids inventing a track record, and moves the conversation to a call, because you never close in an email.

The reply, in substance:
1. "We're property investors and we're new to this local area."
2. "We have access to a lot of properties."
3. "We'd love the opportunity to book a call with you to establish exactly what properties you're currently looking for and where you need them. We can then go out with our network and our resources and find the specific properties, and hopefully we can work together."
4. "Are you free for a call at some point today to discuss further?"

On the question "what councils do you work with": do not dodge and do not invent. Say you are property investors new to the area.

After the call: establish their needs and make sure there is synergy. If they say they are desperate, propose a face-to-face meeting. Email is for finding out more information, never for closing.

#### Shutting down the 'bad council' objection

Also known as: One person, one contact

Some landlords have had a bad experience with the council and refuse to hear the word. Remind them that they are dealing with your company, not the council. Give them one person and one contact, and promise to keep them updated.

When the landlord complains about the council:
"Look, don't worry about that. We'll sort that. You've got one person, one contact, and that's me, and I'll update you."

The point behind it: the landlord's contract is with your company. The council relationship is your problem, not theirs.

Context in the video: a landlord was completely against Luton Council after a bad experience, had held about eight viewings and refused every council tenant.

#### The estate agent pitch: your two options

Also known as: Two options for the agent

This is what Shaun says to an estate agent to win their properties. He frames it as two options for the same commission: lease to him and earn it for nothing, or rent to working professionals and carry all the tenant rights, headaches and staff cost. It is a win for the agent, the landlord, the tenant and you.

Opening: "Look, this is what I do. I do social housing. I'm a social house provider with a local council. I'm looking at scaling. I'm looking at going really big. This is how I can help you. This is how I can make your life easier."

Then the two options:
1. Lease it to me. Zero voids, zero operations, zero risk, because we do not have the same tenant rights as a tenant. Nothing changes for the landlord. Your 12 to 15% stays the same, but now you are paid for doing absolutely nothing.
2. Rent it to a working professional. They have all the tenant rights. Headaches, maintenance, neighbours complaining. You carry that burden for the same 12 to 15%, which means more staff, more manpower, more time and more energy. It is 100% not passive.

Close: why would you want to work harder? It is a win-win-win: the agent gets paid, you get paid, homeless people are housed and the landlord is happy.

Notes: The 12 to 15% is the management fee he says many agents in Birmingham charge landlords.

#### The shop pitch for a new baker

Words Shaun suggests a baker use when walking into a local shop. You introduce yourself, show the range, point to your reviews, and leave free tasters with no pressure. It is written to give value first and make the shop's answer easy.

"Hey, I'm a baker. I bake cakes, and I can do a variety. This is what I've got. I've got some great feedback."

With reviews in hand:
"Look, this is my Google reviews. This is what it is. I'd love the opportunity to sell these cakes in your shop. Here's the tasters. They're free. Try them and let me know what you think."

#### What is this teaching me?

Also known as: Rather than why is this happening to me, Not why is this happening to me

When something goes wrong, swap one question for another. Instead of asking why this is happening to you, ask what this is teaching you. The first question keeps you stuck as a victim; the second makes you look for the lesson that is right in front of you.

- Do not say: why is this happening to me?
- Say instead: what is this teaching me?
- Then try to learn from it and take accountability for your part in it.
- The speaker says this is what he says to himself, and gives it as his final point on handling the dark moments.

- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (49:00)
- R-YT-z6Ei9suvXX0 (08:00)

#### What's happening with all those tenants?

Also known as: Where are you putting those tenants?

When a council tells you it no longer pays nightly rates, or has changed or is overspending, do not hang up. Ask where the tenants are going. The council has to house them somewhere, and that question leads you to the new scheme.

Situation: you call a council and they say something like 'we don't do nightly rates any more, we've changed', or 'we're overspending'.

Say: 'Okay, well what's happening with all those tenants? Where are you putting those tenants?'

Why it works: they have to put them somewhere, so the answer tells you which new scheme, process or landlord opportunity they have created.

Notes: The transcript renders 'nightly rates' as '90 rates' at 1:35:00.

Sources:
- R-YT-PKg8fPvSkfw (1:35:00)

#### When a council says it no longer does temporary accommodation

Also known as: Where will all those people go?

Councils switch temporary accommodation on and off as funding changes, and the person on the phone often just wants the call to end. Do not accept 'we no longer do it' as the end. Ask where the people will go and which schemes are taking them. Often the same council has moved its demand to the AST model.

1. When the council says it no longer pays for or provides temporary accommodation, ask: "Okay, well, where will all those people go?"
2. Point out that funding may vanish but the people do not.
3. Ask what other schemes they are introducing.
- Councils have different criteria and periods when they do and do not provide temporary accommodation.
- Council staff are overworked and understaffed; they want off the phone fast and will not volunteer this, so you must be forefront in asking.
- Do not close the door on a council because of one no.

Notes: The author gives a worked example of a council officer in the temporary accommodation department who, if asked, would pass the caller to a colleague running the AST model where the tenants are going.

Sources:
- R-YT-NuYOOjmh0ZI (15:00)
- R-YT-NuYOOjmh0ZI (14:00)
- R-YT-NuYOOjmh0ZI (16:01)

#### Who are your current approved providers?

When a council tells you it is not taking on new providers, do not hang up. Ask who its current approved providers are, then contact those providers and build a relationship with them. It turns a no into a lead.

1. Call the council.
2. If they say they are not taking on any more providers, ask: "Who are your current approved providers?"
3. Make contact with that provider.
4. Form a relationship with the provider from there, and lease your property to them under private sector leasing.
