#### 1,000 pounds in, 500 pounds a month out

Also known as: Temporary accommodation ROI

Shaun's return-on-investment case for temporary accommodation. If setup costs you 1,000 pounds and the deal pays 500 pounds a month for five years, the outlay is trivial. He uses it to answer the objection that this strategy costs money.

- Put 1,000 pounds into the deal.
- The deal produces 500 pounds every single month for the next 5 years.
- Shaun's verdict: of course it is a great deal; anyone would do it.

- R-YT-fUN_l7UYES4 (16:00)

#### Afraid to spend on a low basic

On commission with a low basic, Shaun would not spend even in good months. He kept thinking: what if I sell nothing next month, what if the market changes? That fear stopped him enjoying the rewards of his hard work, and it is part of why he wanted income that did not reset every month.

- Cause: a very low basic wage in the motor trade, so income depended on each month's sales.
- Thought loop: what happens if next month I do not sell any cars, what if the motor trade or the marketplace changes.
- Effect: he did not want to overstretch himself and could not enjoy his hard work, even on holiday.

Sources:
- R-YT-PKg8fPvSkfw (19:00)

#### Agents bring deals to fast deciders

Shaun has not gone out looking for property deals himself for at least two years. Agents pass deals to him because he built long-term relationships and has a reputation for quick decisions and for not messing people about. Speed is what keeps the network feeding you.

- He built long-term, sustainable relationships with agents and a great network.
- Agents pass him deals because they know he does not mess them about: he makes quick, fast decisions, and the velocity of it all is very important to them.
- Result: he has not needed to source deals himself for at least two years.

#### Car sales as entrepreneurship training

Before property, Shaun sold cars on a low basic salary and high commission. The basic did not cover his bills, so every month he had to sell or go short. He says that pressure was the best preparation for business, because business is constant pressure too.

- Car sales: a low basic wage and high commissions. If you are not a hard worker and do not get up and sell cars, you make no money.
- His basic salary was not enough to cover his bills, so his back was against the wall every month.
- Most people would not like that level of pressure, but business is constant pressure, so when he moved into business he was already used to handling it and the transition was easier.
- Two lessons he carried from car sales into business: the power of showing up and simply doing the work, and the discovery that the best sellers were not the most talented but the most consistent (see those cards).
- He started in business because he wanted to create a financial future for himself and his family and take it into his own hands.

#### Damp from high ground and disconnected drains

Also known as: Bridging the damp

A six-bed new build had serious damp in room one and the kitchen. The cause was outside: the ground had been built up too high against the wall, bridging the damp, and drainpipes were not connected into the gullies, so waste water ran down the side of the house. The room had to be relocated and the ground dug out and re-laid.

What was wrong:
- The ground level outside was too high, bridging the damp and letting dampness into the building
- Drains were not connected into the gullies, so water ran down the side of the property
- The high floor hid the problem: nobody could see that the pipes were not connected
- Damp appeared all along that side, in room one and in the kitchen

Consequences:
- Major issues, a lot of headache, and the tenant in room one had to be relocated to another property

The repair:
1. Dig out the ground to its correct level
2. Connect the pipes into the gullies so waste water is taken away
3. Lay slabs, planned to be finished in one day
4. Big clean up and fixing everything the next day

Lesson Shaun draws: the original build was a terrible job; you need competent people who can come in, dissolve the problem and get it done to a good standard.

Notes: Explained on site by Chris, the contractor doing the repair, with Shaun.

Sources:
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (16:00)
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (17:01)

#### Doing the maths on a first buy-to-let

The calculation that turned Shaun away from the classic buy-to-let route while he was still a car salesman. A 70,000 pound deposit would take six or seven years to save and net only a few hundred pounds a month, and one non-paying tenant could turn that into a monthly loss. It is why he went looking for other structures.

1. Deposit needed for a first buy-to-let: 60,000 to 80,000 pounds.
2. Saving that from a normal job looked like a 6 to 7 year goal, and only possible with a high income.
3. Cash flow from a 70,000 pound investment: about 300 to 400 pounds a month on a good month.
4. If the tenant stops paying and refuses to leave, getting them out could take roughly 6 to 9 months.
5. Conclusion: an asset producing 400 pounds a month could soon become a monthly liability, so he looked at other structures.

Sources:
- R-YT-fUN_l7UYES4 (02:01)
- R-YT-fUN_l7UYES4 (03:01)
- R-YT-fUN_l7UYES4 (04:02)

#### Ed Hughes: £9,000 a month in five and a half months

A student's result from the exact Contracts Finder method. Ed Hughes was a police officer with no property or business experience. He found a provider on Contracts Finder, partnered with them, and within five and a half months was earning £9,000 a month.

- Background: former police officer, zero property experience, zero entrepreneurial skills.

#### Moving to Dubai: who you ask changes the answer

Also known as: The Dubai example

Say you want to move your life and business to Dubai. Someone who has never taken a risk will call it super risky and ask why you would leave your comfort behind. Someone who has done it will tell you how it benefited them and how they did it. Only the second answer lets you make an informed decision.

- The plan: take your life and business, build something in Dubai and start a whole new life there.
- Ask someone who has never taken a risk and you hear: that sounds super risky, why move to the other side of the world when your convenience, comfort and lifestyle are here? They say it because they have never taken risks.
- Ask someone who has done it and been through the motions and you get their true perspective: how it benefited them and how they did it.
- That gives you actual information on what can and will happen for you, not guesses about what might happen.
- Only then can you make an informed decision that is best for you, not for anyone else.

Notes: Shaun speaks of Dubai as "out here", which suggests he is recording there.

#### Rolex versus Casio

A Rolex starts at around £10,000 and a Casio costs around £50, yet both just tell the time. The gap is value perception built by marketing and design. The author uses it to show why a better-marketed product gets paid far more for the same job.

- Rolex: costs on average starting from £10,000. Casio: around £50.
- Both do the same essential job: tell you the time. The kids' answers (it is more luxurious, rich people buy it, for style) all point to perception, not function.
- Rolex created that perception through marketing and clever design.
- The pricing lesson: if you sell your watch for £2,000 and someone else has marketed theirs so it sells at £10,000, you both still sell, but count how many £2,000 watches you have to sell to match one of theirs. Better marketing means you get paid more per sale.

Notes: The watch prices are the author's rough figures ("on average", "I don't know £50"), not researched numbers.

#### Served a statutory demand over £50,000

Also known as: Luigi's darkest moment, Statutory demand

Luigi's darkest moment: a fixed-return investor was owed £50,000, the remortgage ran three weeks late, and the investor's insolvency solicitors served a statutory demand giving 14 days to pay. Luigi sold shares in one of his properties, paid the investor, then completed the remortgage and bought more with the surplus.

- Deal: a private investor on a fixed return, owed £50,000 at the end of the agreement. Luigi had kept him updated that the remortgage was about to complete.
- Three weeks late, the investor went to insolvency solicitors and had a statutory demand served at Luigi's door.
- The demand gave 14 days to pay before court action.
- Fix: sold shares in one of his properties for £50,000 and repaid the investor.
- Afterwards the remortgage came through, leaving extra cash he used to buy more properties.
- Lesson he drew: leverage your own assets rather than borrow more from investors and risk the same thing again. He now has only one very small investor.

Notes: Told by Luigi, not Shaun.

#### The cup of money demonstration

A prop the author uses with his son: a cup holding a small amount of money. It shows how little is actually there, how spending on things you do not need empties it, and that only three activities can fill it back up.

How the demonstration runs:
1. Give the child a bit of money in a cup and ask what they think of it. The point lands on its own: it is not a lot.
2. Explain where that money came from: a job, an investment or a business.
3. Ask what happens if it gets spent on things they do not need. The cup gets emptier and only a little is left.
4. Ask how to fill it up again. Only three things refill it: selling your time in a job, investing for passive income, or creating value with a product or service.
5. Close with the seeds picture: eat them (spend) and they are gone; plant them (invest) and they grow.

Notes: The transcript reads "this is a part of money", most likely "pot"; the prop is called a cup later in the same passage.

#### The pink lemonade stand

The author turns a bottle of pink lemonade into a pretend stand to show value perception at small scale. A plain cup sells for £1 or £2. Better marketing and an added bag of sweets let the same cup sell for £4.

How the demonstration runs:
1. Pour a cup of lemonade, a nice full one, a bit more than you first think: you want the customer to be happy.
2. Treat it as a product on your lemonade stand and charge £1 a cup. In real life you would make the stand look better.
3. Marketing is key. A passer-by who sees a nice, beautiful, colourful stand with colourful writing gets the perception it is worth more, so you could charge more.
4. Take a hypothetical price of £2 a cup and apply the two levers: improve the marketing so the perceived value rises; then add a product, a bag of 10 sweets, and sell lemonade plus sweets for £4, double the price.

Ideas the kids added during the game:
- Selling pink lemonade rather than normal lemonade is a bit different, so people who have not tried it may want to.
- Take £10 to the shop, buy fizzy sweets and sell them alongside the drink. The author confirmed this is exactly the right move.

Notes: The price baseline shifts from £1 in the game to a hypothetical £2 when the author does the doubling maths.

Sources:
- R-YT-OvrW406xkGs (06:00)
- R-YT-OvrW406xkGs (08:00)

#### The Universal Credit phone call

Also known as: No furlough for the newly self-employed, Back against the wall, Universal Credit, Benefits, DSS

Three weeks after Shaun quit BMW, COVID hit. After two hours on hold he was told there was no furlough for someone who had just registered self-employed, only Universal Credit at £75 a week. He turned it down and decided to work his way out. This is the moment his back went against the wall and the social housing business began.

- Everyone at BMW was furloughed; a few more weeks and Shaun would have kept his wage.
- He assumed that having worked since 16 as a good taxpayer, HMRC would pay his wage too.
- He waited about two hours on the phone.
- He explained he had just registered as self-employed, started his business and it had failed, and asked for the furlough money everyone was talking about.
- The answer: Mr Davis, unfortunately there is no furlough for you. Because he had only just registered self-employed, there was no taxable income on record, so no support.
- The only option offered was Universal Credit at £75 a week.
- He refused it: £75 would not scratch the surface of what he was used to earning, and in his words it could have put him in a box.
- Result: back against the wall, no other way out, so he decided he had to figure it out himself.

Notes: The transcript renders 'furlough' as 'furled', 'furowed', 'fur law' and 'fur'. Told as the turning point before the pivot to social housing.

#### The WhatsApp message and the Handsworth six-bed

Also known as: The first social housing deal

Shaun's first social housing deal came from a message in a WhatsApp group he almost never checked. A provider wanted a six-bed in Handsworth, and he had exactly that property on pause. He sourced it to her for a fee, and at the viewing a council officer explained how simple social housing really is.

1. Before the message: working from Tai's room in his small old house, 10 to 15 hours a day, calling councils and telling himself he would not eat until he got a yes.
2. A social housing provider posted in a WhatsApp group that she was looking for a six-bedroom property in Handsworth.
3. He had a six-bed in Handsworth he was meant to close the week before for working professionals and students. He had paused it and the landlord agreed to hold it.
4. Rather than take it on himself, he sourced it to her for a fee, because cash flow was what he was struggling with.
5. At the property he met a council officer who took to him, explained social housing in simple terms and said: why don't you do it, contact that person, contact this, do this, do that.
6. He realised he had been overthinking it the whole time. That one conversation changed his life.
7. He then called the council and became a provider himself.

#### The £10 game: sweets, coaching, Lucozade

An interactive game with the kids that makes the flow of money visible. One £10 note is passed around three people, each buying something the other has to sell. It shows that the same money keeps moving because everyone brought something of value to trade.

How the game ran:
1. Aaliyah starts with £10. She wants the sweets Tyrese has, so she pays him £10 for them.
2. Tyrese wants football coaching from his dad, who charges £10 for it, so he passes the £10 on. The coaching is a service of value.
3. Dad wants the Lucozade Aaliyah has, so he pays her the same £10.
4. Stop and ask what happened: the same £10 moved through three people.
5. Ask why: because each person had something of value to sell, a product (sweets, a drink) or a service (coaching), and each item cost £10.

The point: if you have a product or a service, you become part of the money's journey instead of watching it.

Notes: The transcript spells Lucozade as "Lucas aid", "Lucasade" and "Lucas head". The game can be replayed with any three people and any three items at one price.

**Glossary terms**

#### Back-to-back leasing

Also known as: Back to back lease, Middleman strategy, Back-to-back lease, Rent-to-rent with a back-to-back lease, Source to a provider under a back-to-back lease, Back-to-back leases, Middleman model, Back-to-back leasing is deal sourcing paid monthly, Marry the provider and the landlord, The £3,000 in, £2,200 out, £800 margin deal, Back-to-back leasing margin example, Zero operations strategy, Mediator between provider and landlord

You lease a property from a landlord and lease it straight on to a social housing provider. The provider runs the house and the tenants; you keep the gap between the two rents. The author calls it a game changer because it involves zero operations.

- Definition: find providers (like the author) and become the middleman between landlord and provider
- You take a lease from the landlord, then lease the same property on to the provider
- The key benefit: zero operations, so no property, tenant or management worries
- Income: people doing this make between £500 and £1,000 a month

#### Commercial lease agreement

Also known as: Commercial lease

The contract you sign with the landlord, usually for three to five years. It gives you permission to sublet, which is what lets you lease the property on to a social housing provider. Without it, subletting is illegal, so the contract is not a formality.

- Term: usually three to five years.
- It gives you the right to control the asset and the permission to sublet.
- Subletting without this permission is illegal, so the correct contract is essential.
- With the correct contract in place you control the property as if you owned it, and can then lease it to the provider.

Notes: The transcript reads "you control the properties if you own it", most likely "as if you own it".

- On an HMO basis the incentive is about £600 to £1,000 per room depending on the area.

#### Remedial report

A remedial report is what you get after the council's surveyor has inspected a property. It tells you exactly what needs doing to bring the property up to the council's standard. Your job is to get that work done and then book the surveyor back out.

- When you get it: after the council surveyor has fully inspected the property.
- What it contains: exactly what you need to do to get the property up to standard.
- What you do with it: organise the maintenance on the back end. A viewing company can be on site to make sure the job is done properly and inspect the finished work before you book the surveyor to come back out.
- Once the property passes, you can close the deal.

#### Social housing provider

Also known as: Registered provider, Housing association

A social housing provider is the organisation that sits between you and the council. You supply the property; they supply the tenants, the contract, the support and the guaranteed rent. Knowing who they are is the first step to finding them.

- Types of provider: registered providers, housing associations, charities, CICs, and organisations that deliver accommodation and support for councils
- Their position: the middle point between the council and you
- What you bring: the property
- What they bring: the referrals, the contracts, the support and the guaranteed payments
- Why they matter to you: they can place tenants, pay you guaranteed rent and work with you long term

#### Supported accommodation

Also known as: Supported housing, Two great things about supported accommodation, Why supported accommodation is a viable business, Supported accommodation varies by council, Birmingham only for supported accommodation, What a supported housing provider does, Paid to house and paid to support, Supported accommodation: what it really takes, Support and accommodation, Housing the homeless, Social housing strategy one, Duty of care and liability in supported accommodation, Responsibility when a tenant dies, High-level social housing, Supported living

One of Sean's two social housing strategies. You house homeless people who are unemployed and have a mental health need, and you provide specific support based on their needs, with the aim of moving them to independent living within 6 to 12 months. It is more complex than temporary accommodation, with more red tape.

- Who you house: the homeless, specifically people who are unemployed and have a mental health need.
- Your two responsibilities: house the tenant, and provide support to the tenant. Not random support: specific support based on their needs.
- How: a full risk assessment when the tenant comes in, a tailor-made support plan, and a support worker who visits weekly with that plan.
- The aim: from homeless, to a safe and secure environment in your accommodation, to independent living within 6 to 12 months.
- Complexity: more high level and more red tape than temporary accommodation. It works differently in different councils; Sean only does it in Birmingham.
- Income: paid directly by the government.

#### Temporary accommodation

Also known as: Temporary and emergency accommodation, Temp accommodation, Temporary accommodation with the council, TA, Emergency accommodation, Temporary accommodation scheme, In-between house, Temporary accommodation management, TA management

Housing that a council uses for people and families who cannot get a permanent, stable home. Shaun calls it the buzz word in property right now and the strategy he is using to scale in 2025. You take part by supplying properties the council can place these people in.

1. Local Authority Leasing: lease the property directly to the council for a guaranteed rent over a multi-year term. Also called back-to-back leasing. Zero operations.
2. Private Sector Leasing: lease to housing associations and council approved providers. Also a back-to-back lease with zero operations.
3. Nightly rate scheme: the council pays you a negotiated nightly rate per placement. Higher reward, higher risk, more operations because you manage the tenant directly.

### Also taught as: Temporary accommodation

- When to use: the landlord likes temporary accommodation but is nervous because their mortgage is high
- Sit down together and crunch the numbers
- Offer: your company takes 100% control of the property, looks after the tenant, much of the maintenance and all the payments
- The landlord gets the hands-off experience with a slightly increased rent

Notes: Shaun states at the start that he is not a financial advisor and that the video is not financial advice.

**Procedures**

#### Back your kids' ideas, let them run them

Also known as: Raising kids to build wealth, Giving them the foundation and the framework

Shaun is not giving his children money or assets. He gives them knowledge, a foundation and a framework, then waits for them to bring him ideas. Together they map a game plan and he invests, but the child runs it while he coaches from the background.

1. Teach them the knowledge; give them the foundation and the framework to work with
2. Wait for them to come to you with what they have: "Dad, look, this is what I've got. These are the ideas."
3. Map out the game plan together
4. Invest in the idea and back them 100%
5. They run it and steer the ship
6. You stay in the background to help, support, coach, empower and mentor, making sure they are going in the right direction
- Why: it is far more powerful than handing over a bunch of money and assets, which they end up losing because they never had the hardship
- The aim: when he is gone, he can say hand on heart that they can steer the ship and pass it on to their kids

Notes: He is open that he wants his kids to enjoy the lifestyle he works for; the point is that they must build the skill set alongside it.

Sources:
- R-YT-V0CJIg1RIwc (11:00)
- R-YT-V0CJIg1RIwc (12:02)

#### Council research spreadsheet

Also known as: Council CRM, Find the council with the most demand

Before choosing where to work, track every council you contact and what the local rent is. Comparing them shows which area is most profitable, and that is where you scale.

1. Make a spreadsheet of all the councils you have contacted, or set up a CRM system
2. Add a breakdown of the average rent in each area
3. Compare them: see which council is most profitable and makes the most business sense
4. Scale in that location

#### Finding providers on Contracts Finder

Also known as: Contract Finder, Contractfinder, Contractfinder method, gov.uk/contractsfinder, A contract end date means they must fill it fast, Find providers on Contracts Finder, contractsfinder.gov.uk, Contracts Finder method, contractsfinder.service.gov.uk

Contracts Finder is the government website that lists public contracts and who won them. Shaun filters it for awarded supported living, temporary and emergency accommodation contracts, opens one and reads off every supplier: each is a provider with fresh funding who needs houses right now. He calls it the best way to find a deal, because you are calling proven buyers, not random companies.

1. Go to gov.uk/contractsfinder and click Start.
2. Untick 'early engagement' and 'future opportunity' so you only see awarded contracts. Untick every other status; you only want providers that have actually won.
3. Search a keyword: 'supported living', 'temporary accommodation' or 'emergency accommodation'.
4. Set the location by region or postcode (for example a postcode with a 50 mile radius), or leave it as all locations to see the whole country. All locations showed around 9,000 awarded contracts; not all are relevant, so sift through them. Be as specific as you like about the areas you want to work in.
5. Open a contract. Read the description so you understand what the contract is for and what you would be getting into.
6. Ignore the closing date; it is irrelevant and puts a lot of people off. Check the contract end date instead. A date still in the future (for example 2027) means the contract is active and the provider is likely to be scaling.
8. Copy and paste each supplier name into Google, find the company and its contact number, and get through to the person who can partner with you.
9. Contact them first by phone and introduce yourself, then follow up by email. When you contact them, reference the contract you found them through.
10. Find out whether they are looking to scale. If they are, offer your properties as the solution to their problem.

#### From landlord yes to signed lease

Also known as: Registering a property with the council, Getting the council out to inspect, How Luton Council onboards a property, Getting a property onboarded and into payout

Status: needs review

Once you have a property the council might take, there is a fixed order of events before tenants move in. You send the property to the council and register on its platform, the surveyor inspects, you fix what they flag, the lease is signed and the council places a family. Move fast, because the agent will want a deposit once your offer goes through, and knowing the order lets you keep everyone moving.

1. Straight after the viewing, call your council contact: describe the property (for example a four bed in LU3), ask the best way to send it across and how long inspectors take to come out. Luton said about 5 days.
2. Email immediately, from your phone, before the next agent. Do not wait until you are back at a laptop.
   Email template: "Spoke to my colleague earlier about the temporary accommodation. We've got a fantastic property we think may be of interest to you." Then the property address, then "please, can you let us know next steps?"
3. The council replies with links to register on their platform, the agent and landlord approved contractors list.
4. Once you are registered and the landlord has agreed in principle, book the surveyor. They check for damp and similar problems.
5. On the day, ask the surveyor to give you the list of works in a nutshell so you can start before the written report arrives, usually by the next day.
6. The moment the inspector says the property is good to go, send the landlord a commercial lease agreement the same day. The landlord checks it and signs.
7. Sort the trades and complete the remedial works by the agreed date.
8. The surveyor returns to tick everything off.
9. When the property is ready to hand over keys, it passes to the council's property negotiators, who list it and move somebody in. In the Dave deal the family moved in on the Monday after the Friday re-inspection.

Timing caveat: once your offer to the agent goes through, the agent will want a deposit, so you need the council's response before you commit.

Notes: After the keys, the work is facilitation and leverage. Shaun says you may sometimes be running 10 deals at once and get pulled in every direction; the stress comes with it.

#### Getting your bakes into local shops

Also known as: Tasters, reviews, sale or return, Sale or return

Shaun's step-by-step advice to a tenant starting a cake business on how to get shops to stock it. You make tiny free tasters, walk the row of local shops, lead with what you offer rather than a sales pitch, back it with Google reviews, and take the risk off the shop with sale or return.

1. Make very small taster versions of the product, for example mini muffins.
2. Walk along the local road where there are lots of shops and go in with the tasters.
3. Come from a place of value, not from trying to sell. Introduce yourself as a baker, show the variety you make, mention the good feedback you have had.
4. Set up a Google page for the business first, so customers can leave reviews. Shaun offered to write one himself as a customer. Then collect more from other people.
5. In the shop, show the Google reviews, say you would love the opportunity to sell in their shop, and leave the free tasters for them to try and get back to you.
6. When they come back with a yes, offer terms. Option one: sale or return, no payment up front, leave the product and see if it sells, then agree the price. Option two: state the retail price and the price you sell to the shop for.

#### How to approach a council

Also known as: Forming a partnership with the council, Find the council first, Council first, property second, Council first, Close a high-demand council first, then hunt properties, Local authority first, then the property, The formula, Council before property, Start with the council, Becoming a temporary accommodation provider, Temporary and emergency accommodation, TEA

Every council has a housing sector, and inside it a temporary and homeless accommodation department. That department is your way in. You call it, learn what they need, then go and find it and offer it back to them as a partnership.

1. Find the temporary and homeless accommodation department inside the council's housing sector. Every council has one.
2. Call that department. Find out exactly what the demand is, what the tenant type is and exactly what they are looking for.
3. Ask the three core questions: which areas they want to scale in, which property types they want, and what they pay.
4. Go out and find exactly what they need.
5. Match the two together and form a partnership with the council.
6. Throughout, be very clear about what you can do and what your intentions are, so they take you seriously.

#### How to earn your team's respect

Also known as: Win-win leadership, Vested interest

Shaun earns respect in two moves: he bends over backwards for his team, and he works out what each person wants and where he fits into that. When a team member can see the value you create for them, they work harder because you both have a stake in the outcome.

1. Bend over backwards for your team
2. Get a clear understanding of each person's goals and motives and what they are looking to achieve
3. Work out how and where you can position yourself in helping them get there
Why it works, from a business point of view:
- If a team member clearly sees the value you create for them and how you help them achieve their goals, they end up working harder
- You both have a vested interest: they work for you, you help them reach their goals
- It is a win-win: everyone is winning and everyone is motivated, instead of you just directing and calling the shots

#### How to run a viewing as a temporary accommodation provider

Also known as: Viewing checklist, Viewing checks: damp, ventilation and remedial work

At a viewing you are checking the house against what the council will demand and testing whether the landlord will do a company let. Ask a few pointed questions, look for the usual surveyor items such as damp, window restrictors and doors, and give the agent a clear next step. Damp is usually a ventilation habit, not a building fault, and minor works are fine as long as they are not expensive.

1. Ask the agent: "How long has this property been empty?" and "Have you had much interest?"
2. Count the reception rooms. A second one can potentially become a bedroom.
3. Look for any sign of damp or mould; the council will be all over it. Ask who is dealing with it (here the property management team).
   - Common cause: no ventilation. No extractor fan in the bathroom and windows kept shut while showering, especially in the cold.
   - Fixes: fit a fan first; keep the window slightly open; test any existing fan actually works.
4. Check whether windows already have restrictors. Each one present is one less job.
5. Open and close every door; a door that does not close will be flagged.
6. Ask: "Is the landlord open to a company let on this one?"
7. Ask the price and whether the landlord is open to any negotiation on the rent.
8. Close with the agent: "If you can put it to the landlord for us and let him know exactly what we do, let us know what you need from us and we'll take it from there."

Accept a bit of remedial work if nothing is too expensive or impossible. The council surveyor will also ask for minor works before handover.

Notes: Part of the viewing dialogue is garbled in the transcript.

#### How to set up a back-to-back lease

Also known as: Setting up a back-to-back lease deal, Back-to-back leasing, Back-to-back lease, Three-way deal

The steps for the middleman route. Learn what a provider wants, find a property that fits, agree a rent with the landlord below what the provider pays, then connect the two and keep the difference.

1. Call a provider and ask the three core questions: which areas, which property types, what rent they pay
2. Get a clear understanding of what they need
3. Go out and find a property that matches
4. Negotiate a rent with the landlord that is lower than what the provider will pay
5. Marry the two together: lease from the landlord, lease on to the provider
6. Your profit is the gap between the two rents; you do not manage the property or the tenants

#### Know the goal, work out the price, pay it

Also known as: Brian Tracy's Goals formula, Get busy paying the price

From Brian Tracy's book Goals, Shaun takes a three-step recipe: be clear on what you want, work out what it will cost you, then get busy paying that price. His own version is to understand the sacrifice, do it, and stay consistent until it pays off.

Brian Tracy's steps as Shaun quotes them:
1. Work on your goal: have a clear understanding of what you want to achieve.
2. Work out the price you will need to pay.
3. Get busy paying the price. Shaun calls this his favourite part.

Shaun's own reading of it:
1. Understand what sacrifice you will need to make.
2. Just do it.
3. Stay consistent and run the course of the game.
4. Only then will you succeed.

#### Mentally resetting after taking an L

Also known as: Bouncing back from a loss, How to reframe an L

You will take losses in business, and a lot of them. Sean's two-step reset: first look at your own part, because that is the only thing you can change, then ask what the loss is teaching you. The faster you extract the lesson, the faster you bounce back.

1. Look at your own part first. Do not point the blame at everyone else; that is not being accountable. If you blame the other person you cannot change anything, because you can only change what you do. Find where you went wrong, change it straight away and do better next time. That puts the outcome back in your control.
2. Ask: what is this teaching me? Not: why is this happening to me? Every L carries a lesson.
3. Extract the lesson quickly. The quicker you extract it, the quicker you bounce back.

Underlying idea: perspective is everything. When you change the way you look at things, things begin to change.

Sources:
- R-YT-z6Ei9suvXX0 (07:00)
- R-YT-z6Ei9suvXX0 (08:00)

#### Onboarding as a council provider

Becoming a provider is a form-filling step, not a pitch. The council sends an onboarding form, you complete it, and they run checks that focus on fraud against the company directors because the contract is government-backed. Ask the three core questions before this stage.

1. Ask the three core questions first: areas of demand, property types, rent settings; onboarding is a later stage
2. The council sends you an onboarding form
3. Fill out the form and you become a provider
4. The council runs checks: mainly fraud checks on you personally and on the directors of the company
5. A history of fraud will make onboarding very hard, because it is a government-backed contract
6. A new company with no turnover is fine; what counts is the value you bring

#### Pitching social housing to landlords

Also known as: How to pitch social housing, Closing landlords, Convincing a landlord who says no subletting, Landlord objections, Mortgaged landlords

Sean's order of play when he sits down with a landlord. Build rapport until they like and trust you, make them believe that you believe in what you sell, get them to lay out their pain points, then present your service as the solution and spell out the benefits: government backed, guaranteed rent, no hassle.

1. Build rapport before anything else. Doing business is about them understanding you, liking you, trusting you and having the confidence that you are the right person for the job. Sean cannot teach you how to find common ground because every person has different interests: find out theirs and relate them back to you so you are relatable.
2. Get them to believe in you. People do not need to believe what you are saying; they need to believe that you believe it. That shows up as conviction, passion and enthusiasm, and enthusiasm sells.
3. Help the landlord see your service as the solution to their problem. Start by extracting their pain points: what has their experience in property been, why are they now interested in social housing, why is it a viable option for them now. Once they have laid out their pain points, sell your service as the solution to those points.
4. Tell them the viable benefits: what social housing is, how it works, that it is government backed and that they get guaranteed rent. Most landlords want a profitable, zero-hassle solution, so show how your service provides exactly that.

Sean points to a filmed challenge where he lived with a stranger, Sandu, for a week and got him his first deal from start to finish, as the in-depth version of this process.

#### Rent to HMO strategy

Also known as: Rent to rent, Rent to HMO, The guaranteed rent letter to landlords, Guaranteed rent pitch, HMO, Shared house

Rent to HMO is how Shaun started in property in 2018. You find landlords who struggle to rent rooms because the property is dated, lease it from them for three to five years, refurbish it and rent it out at a higher rate. The gap between the two rents, £700 to £1,000 a month per property, is your profit.

1. Find landlords who have a problem renting out their rooms, usually because the rooms are dated.
2. Show them the problem: the property is old and no one wants it, so they are struggling to rent it.
3. Lease the property from the landlord on a long-term rent, around 3 to 5 years.
4. Refurbish the property to make it better. Shaun's spend is given in the transcript as £45,000.
5. Because you have added value, put an increase on the rent.
6. Make between £700 and £1,000 a month on the property.

Context: Shaun discovered rent to rent through a course he says was not that good but taught him the fundamentals. He ran the strategy alongside his full-time job at BMW and says it worked very well in 2018.

Notes: The refurbishment figure '£45,000' comes from an auto-transcript and is ambiguous; it may be a garbled smaller range. Check before relying on it.

#### Rent-to-rent temporary accommodation, step by step

Also known as: How the temp accommodation model works

Status: needs review

The model Shaun has repeated over 90 times. You take a property from a landlord, guarantee their rent, and hand the property to a council for temporary or emergency accommodation. The council pays you either a nightly rate or a lease.

1. Find the deal: build relationships with letting agents and landlords.
2. Take the property on and guarantee the rent to the landlord.
3. Give the property to the council for temporary or emergency accommodation.
4. Get paid one of two ways, depending on the council's scheme: a negotiated nightly rate per tenant, or a lease of the property to the council.
- Councils run different temporary accommodation schemes, so the deal depends on which council you work with.

#### Supporting a tenant from homeless to independent

Also known as: Risk assessment and support plan, Supported accommodation tenant process

The steps Sean's organisation follows with each supported accommodation tenant. Assess the risks and needs on arrival, write a plan made for that person, work it weekly with a support worker, and aim to have them living independently in 6 to 12 months.

1. When the tenant comes in, do a full risk assessment.
2. Make sure they understand exactly what their needs are and how you are going to support them.
3. Create a support plan for that tenant. It is tailor made, not generic.
4. The support worker goes into the tenant with that plan every week to support them.
5. Goal: move them from homeless, into your accommodation as a safe and secure environment, and back out into independent living within 6 to 12 months. Sean also describes working on their mindset and skill set to get them back into employment in the same window.

Why the stop gap is needed: going straight from high mental health need and unemployment back into society is, most of the time, not viable.

Sources:
- R-YT-z6Ei9suvXX0 (13:01)

#### The temporary accommodation deal journey

Also known as: A deal from start to finish, Viewing to tenant

This is the path of a rent-to-rent temporary accommodation deal from first viewing to a tenanted property, as Shaun walks it. Each step shows what you must do and what a third party can do for you. Knowing the sequence lets you plan your few focused hours around the moments that matter.

1. View the property. Do it yourself or have a viewing company do it at a couple of hours' notice; they send a video, comments on the condition and lots of pictures for around 35 to 40.
2. If you are happy with it, contact the council. The council arranges a surveyor to come out and fully inspect the property.
3. Attend the surveyor's inspection yourself if you can. This is where you build relationships with the council and the surveyors. If you are not available, the viewing company can attend on your behalf.
4. After the inspection you receive a remedial report. It tells you exactly what you need to do to bring the property up to standard.
5. Get the remedial work done. You organise it on the back end; the viewing company can be there to make sure the job is done properly and can inspect the work before you book the surveyor back out.
6. Once the property passes, close the deal and get the agreement boxed off on the back end.
7. Put a lock box on the property so contractors can come and go without key problems. The viewing company can collect the keys from the estate agent or landlord for you.
8. Once the tenant moves in, inspect fortnightly at first, or even weekly, and taper to monthly and then quarterly as the tenant proves reliable. The viewing company can do these inspections too.

#### A commercial lease sidesteps the Renters Rights Bill

Also known as: Rent under your company, not your name

The heart of Shaun's pitch. When a landlord signs a commercial lease agreement with your company, the Renters Rights Bill does not apply, because the property is not anyone's principal residence and the tenant is a company, not a person. You give the landlord back the long-term certainty the Bill took away.

- Sign a company let agreement, a commercial lease agreement, with the landlord.
- The Renters Rights Bill does not come into effect because the property is not a prime principal residence.
- You are not renting as an individual; your company is the tenant.
- Shaun presents this as a great selling point: it restores the long-term profitable solution landlords want and turns their pain point into your opening.

#### A company is easier to get out than a tenant

Also known as: Tenant act does not apply

Michael tells landlords that his company does not live in the property, so the law that protects residential tenants does not apply to it. That makes a company easier to remove than an ordinary tenant, which is one more reason a deposit is not needed.

- The property is not the company's own residence; nobody from the company lives there
- So the tenant act does not apply and the company does not have the same tenant rights
- A landlord can get a company out much more easily than a tenant
- Used as an argument for not paying a deposit

#### Care providers are not good at finding property

Providers are experts at care and support, not at sourcing houses. That is a different skill, and most of them struggle with it. If you can find and source properties for them, you solve a real problem and can grow your portfolio alongside them.

- Observation from social housing events: care providers are great at providing care but not necessarily at finding properties
- Finding property is a different skill set altogether
- The opportunity for you: solve that problem by finding and sourcing properties for them
- The payoff: you help them scale, and you grow your own portfolio on the back of it

#### Council money means you will get paid

Shaun's reason for trusting council-backed income: it is the council's money, and a council will not let you work with one of its third parties and go unpaid. That is how he recovered the £60,000 a failed franchise owed him.

- The council made good on the debt because the money was theirs and the work had been done for them.

Social housing providers get paid to house the tenant and paid again to support the tenant. Because they make money on both sides, they can afford to pay you a bit above market rent to get properties quickly. This is why a back-to-back margin exists.

- With most social housing strategies the provider is paid to house the tenant and paid to support the tenant.
- Making money from both ends means they are willing to pay a bit of a premium to acquire the property.
- Companies looking to scale pay a premium to acquire properties fast, because more properties mean more revenue for them.

#### Upfront incentives solve the cash-flow problem of scaling

The hardest part of growing a new business is cash flow: you put money in up front and wait to get it back before you can do the next deal. When the council pays an incentive at the start, you skip that wait. That is why this strategy can scale quickly.

- Most new businesses struggle with cash flow: you pay a lot up front and must wait to pull your initial capital back out before you can scale
- In this strategy the council can pay an incentive up front (in the case study, £9,000)
- Having that cash at the start gives you the upper hand to scale quickly
- Sean says he has many students closing deals this way month after month

On nightly rate properties, Shaun finds that an empty room does not stay empty for long. When one tenant moves out, the council usually places another within three to five working days. That is because councils are flooded with people they must house.

- Observation from Shaun's own nightly rate properties.
- When a tenant moves out, another is usually moved in within three to five working days.

- Most landlords want a profitable, zero-hassle solution.

**Mistakes**

#### "I'm your boss, do as I say"

Also known as: Demanding respect, Calling the shots

In the corporate world, Shaun says, bosses assume people come to work for them and must do as they are told. That does not earn respect, and without care the team only performs when you are watching. Respect is earned, not demanded.

- The mistake: thinking people come to work for you, and that being the boss means they must do as you say
- Why it fails: it is not an effective way to gain respect; you do not demand respect, you earn it
- The consequence: when you just direct and call the shots there is no care, and the team works only while you are watching
- The fix: earn respect by bending over backwards for your team and helping them reach their goals

#### Assuming social housing means smashed windows

Landlords hear social housing and picture high-need tenants, kicked-in doors and smashed windows. Temporary accommodation is not that. It is ordinary families who are grateful for a home and want to settle down. Correcting this picture is part of winning landlords over.

- The fear: high-level tenants, doors kicked in, windows smashed.
- The reality in temporary accommodation: normal families on the council register, usually with young kids, who want to get settled.
- Fix: explain the tenant type to the landlord. Nine times out of ten there is no hassle, though issues can arise wherever there are people.

#### Being a different person online and in real life

Also known as: Who are you?

Some property people portray one character on social media and turn out to be someone else when you meet them. The speakers call this out because it breaks trust the moment someone meets you. The fix is simple: only show the person you actually are.

- The pattern: you follow someone on social media or watch their YouTube, you connect with them, then you meet them in real life and they are completely different.
- The speaker says he has seen a lot of this in the property world, and his reaction on meeting them is: who are you?
- Fix: make how you are online exactly how you are in person, so nobody can ever say that about you.

Sources:
- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (43:02)

#### Being too transactional on the phone

Sandu's calls went straight to 'when can I view?'. That is efficient but forgettable, and it does not work on everyone. Open with a greeting and a bit of small talk so the other person lowers their guard and remembers you.

- Mistake: opening with your name and the request for a viewing time, nothing else
- Consequence: the call feels transactional; you sound like everyone else and are easy to forget
- Fix: say hi and ask how they are, mention something like the weather, bring more energy; it feels out of character at first but it lowers their guard and makes you memorable
- Direct and to the point is fine for some people, but not for everyone; when you want to be remembered, make it less transactional

Sources:
- R-YT-HJ5NOvSeCDA (1:20:00)

#### Borrowing from Peter to pay Paul

Also known as: Raising new finance to repay old investors

Some investors raise 6 or 12 month finance knowing the project will not finish in time, then raise new money to repay the old investor. Shaun calls it the worst thing you can do: you stack private debt on top of mortgage debt, and if the next raise fails, an investor who trusted the deal goes unpaid.

- The pattern: short-term investor finance, project runs over, repay by raising more finance from someone else. Some people have built whole portfolios this way.
- Why it is dangerous: you are in massive debt, mortgage debt plus all the private investor debt. Many in the industry end up not paying investors back at all.
- The investor is relying on the deal completing to be repaid from the deal, not on your ability to find another lender.
- Shaun challenged a guest on their podcast who did this; his answer was that he would not be able to sleep at night. The stress of knowing you cannot pay people back can ruin a life.
- Fix: match the loan term to the realistic project timeline with a buffer, or use a JV partnership where nothing has to be repaid.

- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (27:01)

#### Buying at auction on a 30 to 60 day completion

Also known as: Don't do auction

Auction purchases must complete in 30 to 60 days, and if you miss it you lose your 10% deposit. Luigi steers his refurbishment students away unless the funds are already sitting there. The safer route is to buy a lot that failed to sell at auction and negotiate your own timeline, such as three months to complete.

- The pressure: 30 to 60 days to complete and get the keys. The searches and paperwork are usually done, but the time pressure is severe.
- The cost of missing: they keep your 10% deposit.
- Only consider it if you have the funds readily available, or are certain you can raise the finance in time. Hoping to raise it afterwards is very risky.
- Fix: buy something that did not sell at auction and negotiate terms directly, for example 'I need three months to complete'.
- Luigi's stance to students who insist: if you want to risk it, risk it, but his advice is do not do auctions.

Notes: Luigi's advice to students on the buy-refurbish-refinance side of the academy.

#### Chasing money instead of the fundamentals

Also known as: Money is a byproduct of the work, Get your 10,000 hours in

When Shaun was young and broke he focused only on making money, and made none. The mistake was skipping the fundamentals. Put your energy into building the skill and becoming a true expert, and the money comes as a byproduct.

- The mistake: being money focused because you have no money, and asking only what you can do to earn money.
- The consequence: he stayed financially broke and could not catch a break, because his mindset and focus were on the wrong things.
- The fix: put your energy into building the skill. Get your 10,000 hours in, hone the process, understand your product and your service, and become a true expert in your field.
- Then everything falls into place. The money and the compensation are a byproduct of the work you put in.

- The mistake: comparing your step one to someone else's step twenty on social media.
- The consequence: you get discouraged, and some of what you are comparing yourself to is not even real.

#### Confusing a commercial lease with an AST

Also known as: Rent-to-rent is illegal myth

People who call rent-to-rent illegal are judging it as if it were an ordinary tenancy. An assured shorthold tenancy is an individual tenancy for someone's home. A commercial lease agreement is a company let, a different contract with different rules. Mixing the two up leads to wrong conclusions about what is allowed.

- The mistake: viewing rent-to-rent through the lens of an assured shorthold tenancy agreement. That is an individual tenancy, not a company let agreement.
- Fix: sign, and think in terms of, a commercial lease agreement. That contract makes the arrangement legal and gives you full control to operate the property.
- Shaun's rule of thumb: he is not renting as an individual, he is renting under his company.

Sources:
- R-YT-JygqeccwlQU (04:01)

#### Doing every job yourself

Also known as: Fixing the plumbing and painting the walls yourself

The trap Shaun fell into when he started. A plumbing problem meant he drove to the property to fix it himself; a new property meant he painted it himself. Doing everything kept him busy and kept the business small.

- The mistake: handling every task personally. His examples: going to a property to figure out a plumbing issue himself, and painting a newly taken property himself.
- The consequence: the business could not grow while he was doing everything, and he admits he really struggled to stop.
- The fix: let go to grow. Hire experienced people at every level, then manage and empower them so you can lead and grow the business instead. That was when the business started to grow.

Sean tried to be his own bookkeeper in year one to save costs. His accounts were a mess, year end was a nightmare and the accountant charged him more to redo everything from scratch. The fix is simple: get a bookkeeper or accountant from day one.

- The error: trying to do everything yourself and penny pinch on costs, including inputting your own books without knowing what you are doing.
- The consequences Sean hit: accounts in a mess at the end of his first year; an absolute nightmare trying to figure things out; the accountant charging more because he had to go through all the books and redo everything from scratch because it was all wrong.
- The bigger cost: in the long run the penny pinching sabotaged his success and slowed him down.

- Consequence: you need an unrealistically cheap property, and deals do not stack up

#### Signing a contract and missing one clause

Also known as: 15 pages of jargon

Beginners get excited and sign, assuming it will be fine. It is not fine when something goes wrong and the landlord points to the contract. One missed clause in 15 pages of jargon can cost you thousands of pounds.

The mistake:
- Contracts carry a lot of unnecessary jargon, and people can put things in different clauses.
- If you are new to the game it is easy to miss, and you may sign in excitement thinking it will be okay.
- When something goes wrong, the landlord will say: let's look at the contract, what did you sign?
- One missed clause in 15 pages could cost you thousands of pounds.
The fix:
- Read every contract through properly before signing.
- Have someone with experience check it over first; the hosts do this for every deal in their academy.

#### Signing an AST in your own name and subletting

Also known as: Illegal subletting

This is the version of rent-to-rent that really would be illegal, and the one critics picture. If you rent a house as yourself on a normal tenancy and sublet it, you break your contract. The fix is structural: your company signs a commercial lease that allows subletting.

- The mistake: signing an assured shorthold tenancy with the landlord as an individual and then subletting.
- Consequence: that would be illegal; it is what people mean when they say rent-to-rent is illegal.
- Fix: rent as a company, not as you, under a commercial lease agreement that permits subletting.
- The confusion is a lack of education: critics have read their own residential contract, which says you cannot sublet, and assume every contract says the same.

#### Skipping a networking event because it is far

Also known as: How badly do you want it?

Shaun hears people say they skipped an event because it was on the other side of the country. His reply: how badly do you want success? One contact at that event could be a life-changing opportunity, and seen that way the trip is always worth it.

- The mistake: not going to a networking event because of the distance.
- The question to ask yourself: how badly do you really want the success?
- The reframe: at that event you could make one contact that gives you a life-changing opportunity.
- Looked at through that lens, the event becomes worth going to.

Sources:
- R-YT-eutIKciI3fo (05:00)

#### Skipping due diligence on the tenant or provider

Whoever ends up living in the property, you are the one who signed the contract with the landlord. If the property gets trashed, the landlord holds you responsible, not the council or the provider. So vet the tenant or the housing provider before you hand over the keys.

- Applies to all three schemes.
- The mistake: not doing due diligence on the tenant or on the housing provider you lease to.
- The consequence: if the property gets trashed, it is your responsibility, because you signed the contract with the landlord and the landlord will hold you responsible.
- The fix: do your due diligence on the tenant or the housing provider before placing or leasing.

Sources:
- R-YT-dOkuUCpqijQ (07:00)

#### Spending on things you don't need

Spending money on things you do not need drains your pot until almost nothing is left. The author's first lesson is to understand how money works instead of just spending it.

- The error: spending what you have on things you do not need.
- The consequence: the cup gets emptier and you are left with only a little.
- The fix: understand how money works before you spend it, refill the cup through a job, an investment or a business, and treat money as seeds to plant rather than eat.

Sources:
- R-YT-OvrW406xkGs (01:02)
- R-YT-OvrW406xkGs (08:00)

#### Spending the money when big numbers arrive

When the first big payments hit the account, many people get excited and start spending. Shaun warns that this can make a business collapse very quickly. Longevity matters more than the buzz.

- The mistake: seeing big numbers come in and spending all the money.
- Consequence: the business can collapse very quickly.
- Fix: keep rolling the money back into the business and aim for longevity.

Sources:
- R-YT-bqJS1k17GVo (10:00)
- R-YT-bqJS1k17GVo (11:01)

#### Spending your scarce time on low value tasks

Status: needs review

The mistake Shaun sees most entrepreneurs make: they say they have no time, then spend the time they do have on research and emails. Having little time is fine. The fix is to make sure every hour you do spend in the business is on high value tasks.

- The mistake: focusing on low value tasks, then giving excuses about not having enough time.
- Why it matters: when you only have one day a week, that day has to move the needle or you stay stuck in the job.
- The fix: accept the time limit, outsource the low value work, and spend your own time only on high value tasks such as viewings, appointments and key calls.

Sources:
- R-YT-PKg8fPvSkfw (26:01)

#### Spoiling your kids

Giving children everything feels like love, but Shaun says the word tells you the result: spoil them and they become spoiled. You skip the lessons and the hardship, so you do more damage than good. Let them enjoy the fruits, but teach the skill set alongside.

- The mistake: spoiling your kids too much
- The consequence: they become spoiled, you are not teaching the lessons, and you do more damage than good
- Why it backfires: without hardship they end up losing what they are given
- The fix: be conscious about it; show them the lifestyle and let them enjoy the fruits of your labour, but teach them so they build the skill set to steer the ship when you are gone

Sources:
- R-YT-V0CJIg1RIwc (13:00)

#### Taking advice from people who have not done it

Also known as: Be careful who you get your advice from, Asking for business advice from people without a business, Advice from the wrong people

Most people ask friends and family for advice about goals those people have never reached. Shaun says that is like asking someone who is not a doctor for medical advice. They cannot advise on what they have not been through, so you get the wrong advice.

- The mistake: getting advice from the wrong people, usually friends and family
- Why it fails: they have never been through what you want to achieve, so they cannot advise on it
- His example: if your parents are not property entrepreneurs, do not ask them for property advice
- His comparison: seeking medical advice from someone who is not a doctor
- The fix: get advice from people who have done what you want to do, for example by following property entrepreneurs if you want to be one

Sources:
- R-YT-V0CJIg1RIwc (08:01)
- R-YT-eutIKciI3fo (10:00)

#### Taking business advice from loved ones

Also known as: Speak to someone who is where you want to be, Get them to mentor you

Your parents love you, but if they have never run a successful business their business advice will be wrong. Shaun's fix is simple: go to someone who has done exactly what you want to do. Want to be a property investor? Find a property investor, ask how they started and ask them to mentor you.

- The mistake: mistaking your loved ones for good advisers. If your parents are not entrepreneurs and have no successful business, do not go to them for business advice. You still love them; they will just give you the wrong advice.
- The fix:
  1. Identify someone who is where you want to be and speak to that person.
  2. Go to someone who has a successful business, ideally in your field. If you want to be a property investor, go and speak to a property investor.
  3. Ask them how they started and get them to mentor you.
  4. Find the network: social media, and the many business and property networking events. There is a networking event for your niche.
  5. Surround yourself with that group.

Sources:
- R-YT-nuKBUiE3Afc (15:01)

#### The 5 a.m. myth

Also known as: You don't need to get up at 5 a.m.

Online gurus say you must get up at 5 a.m. to succeed. Shaun says that is a myth. As an entrepreneur you choose your hours; what matters is that the hours you work are productive, not what time the alarm goes off.

- The claim you will see online: successful people get up at 5 a.m., so you must too.
- Shaun's view: there is no need to get up at 5 a.m. to be successful. The privilege of being an entrepreneur is working your own hours.
- The fix: whatever hours you work, be productive in them and move the needle on the business (see productive vs busy).

Sources:
- R-YT-O1krmmSq5Zo (14:00)

#### Thinking you know it all

Also known as: The day you know it all is the day of your downfall

Shaun warns that the moment you believe you know everything is the moment you start to sink. It is the beginning of a decline, not a peak. The fix is to keep the student mindset no matter how far you get.

- The mistake: deciding you have learned enough and know it all.
- The consequence: that day is your downfall; you begin sinking and end up in decline.
- The fix: stay a student to the game and keep investing in your education.

Sources:
- R-YT-eutIKciI3fo (01:00)

#### Thinking your own boss means the beach

Also known as: Leave the 9 to 5, be prepared for the 5 to 9, You always have someone to answer to

People imagine being their own boss as lounging around with nobody to answer to. Sean says it is the exact opposite. You swap the 9 to 5 for the 5 to 9, and you answer to your team, your landlords, the council and your residents.

- The error: expecting that running your own business means chilling on the beach.
- The reality: the saying Sean quotes is, if you want to leave your 9 to 5, be prepared for the 5 to 9.
- You never reach a point with nobody to answer to. Sean answers to his team, to landlords, to the council, and to his residents as a duty of care. He has to keep the cogs turning.
- Being a boss is not for everyone. It can be very stressful, so you need to understand how you deal with that stress.

- R-YT-z6Ei9suvXX0 (05:02)
- R-YT-PKg8fPvSkfw (04:01)

#### Trusting builders you have not checked

Also known as: Watch your builders

When builders work on your property, poor work can be hidden the moment a floor goes down, and you only find out when damp appears later. Shaun's rule: watch them, and make sure they are competent rather than just talking a good game. Have competent people you can call to put problems right.

- Mistake: leaving builders to it and taking their word that the job is right. Consequence: they can hide poor work; once the floor is up you cannot see what is underneath, and the damage shows later as damp, a relocated tenant and a lot of headache.
- Fix: watch builders while they are in your properties and check that they are competent, not just bragging.
- Fix: keep competent people around who can come in, dissolve the problems and get the work done to a good standard.

- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (17:01)
- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (18:01)

#### Turning down deals under £1,000 a month

Shaun has seen people refuse deals that pay less than £1,000 a month. Michael's two neighbouring properties make about £550 combined and he took them because they cost him nothing. Fix: weigh the time and money in, and the life you change, not just the number.

- Some people turn down anything under £1,000 a month
- Michael's deals range from over £1,000 down to £500, and two neighbouring properties together bring about £550
- Fix: if the deal takes little time and no money, it is worth having
- A deal is not always about the numbers; it is also about the life you change, and then you move quickly on to the next

#### Victim mentality

Also known as: Victim mindset, Why does this always happen to me, No victim mentality, Keep the drive

The victim mentality is the story that nothing ever goes your way, all your businesses flop and your breakthrough never comes. Shaun and Luigi call it one of the worst mindsets a person can have: it attracts more negativity, and while you ask those questions you are not growing, because the lesson is right in front of you and you cannot see it. The fix is to focus on what you want, take control of what you can, and ask what the situation is teaching you.

- Signs: 'nothing ever goes my way', 'things don't work out for me', 'all my businesses flop', 'why does this always happen to me', 'why am I not getting my breakthrough'.
- Effect: it attracts more negativity, in line with the idea that what you focus on grows.
- Cost: while you ask those questions you are not growing, because you are not looking. The lesson is right in front of you but you are not able to see it.
- Fix: focus on what you want and take control of what you can. Ask 'what is this teaching me?', learn from it, and take accountability for what you did or did not put in place.

Sources:
- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (16:00)
- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (17:01)
- R-YT-Lr_OKMBndUo (49:00)

- R-YT-Y3K2aTVc9XM (08:01)

#### Waiting for all your ducks in a row

Also known as: Waiting for the perfect time, Waiting for your ducks to be in a row, Waiting for the perfect time to invest

Many entrepreneurs will not start until everything is lined up. Shaun says things are never going to be perfect, so waiting for the perfect time means waiting forever. Start with what you have.

- The mistake: wanting all your ducks in a row before taking action. Shaun sees it in new and experienced entrepreneurs alike.
- The consequence: you wait forever, because things are never perfect.
- The fix: take imperfect action now, with what you have, where you are, with who you know and the knowledge you have.

#### Waiting for it to land on your plate

Also known as: The recipe is there, you have to cook it, How badly do you want it, How badly do you want it?

The biggest problem Shaun sees in aspiring entrepreneurs: they want success handed to them. The knowledge is free on YouTube, Instagram and TikTok, so you can become an expert in anything. But the recipe only works if you mix it and cook it yourself, and that comes down to how badly you want it.

- The mistake: wanting success to land on your plate. People have the desire but are not willing to do the work.
- The reality: you can become an expert on anything, because so many people give valuable content on YouTube, Instagram and TikTok.
- The fix: the recipe is there, but mixing it and cooking it is your job. Ask yourself how badly you actually want it.

#### Luton Council temporary accommodation rates

Also known as: Luton Council nightly rates and demand

Status: needs review

These are the numbers Luton Council gave in June 2025 for temporary accommodation. You use them to work out whether a rental in the town leaves a profit. They are not negotiable and every agent gets the same.

- Rates are not negotiable; the same for every agent.

#### Startup costs for rent-to-rent social housing

Also known as: Setup costs, Being put off by small setup costs

Costs to start depend on the strategy, but the basics are small. Around £300 covers registering the business and a professional image, and insurance runs about £75 a month for a small business. Back-to-back leasing adds nothing on top, because you are not running the property.

- Business setup, all in: about £300. Covers registering the company, a domain, a professional email address and a virtual telephone number so you have a proper landline rather than a mobile.
- Insurance, all policies together, when the business is small: about £75 a month, or roughly £500 to £600 if paid up front for the year.
- Back-to-back leasing: no further startup costs, since you are only finding and providing the property. You still need professional indemnity and public liability insurance.
- Also recommended: membership of the PRS, the redress scheme.
- Most insurances can be bought after the first deal brings money in.

Notes: Said by Luigi, the co-host.

Sources:
- R-YT-NuYOOjmh0ZI (17:00)
- R-YT-NuYOOjmh0ZI (18:00)
- R-YT-NuYOOjmh0ZI (19:01)

## Glossary

- Back-to-back leasing: You lease a property from a landlord and lease it straight on to a social housing provider. The provider runs the house and the tenants; you keep the gap between the two rents. The author calls it a game changer because it involves zero operations.
- BRRRR method: The four-step route to buying and keeping property that Shaun names as the way to own assets: buy, refurbish, refinance, rent. He calls it game-changing for building wealth. In this video he only names it; the detail goes to the cash flow strategies.
- Commercial lease agreement: The contract you sign with the landlord, usually for three to five years. It gives you permission to sublet, which is what lets you lease the property on to a social housing provider. Without it, subletting is illegal, so the contract is not a formality.
- Creating value in the marketplace: The author's phrase for running a business: you make a product or a service that people want or need, and they pay you for it. It is the third way money comes in and the one the whole video builds toward.
- HMO: An HMO is a house with several unrelated people living in it, each usually renting a room. In this model the incentive is paid per room, so an HMO with more rooms brings a bigger upfront payment, but you also carry the bills.
- HMO (house share): An HMO, as Shaun uses the word, is a house share. Several tenants rent rooms in one house and share the bathroom and the kitchen. Most of his rooms before COVID were HMOs, which is why lockdown hit him so hard.
- Ignorance tax: The price you pay for not knowing what you do not know. The example is a student who spent a year going nowhere alone, then closed within a month once shown the steps. Shaun links it to a plain test: if you are not making £5,000, £10,000 or £15,000 a month, there is a reason.
- Remedial report: A remedial report is what you get after the council's surveyor has inspected a property. It tells you exactly what needs doing to bring the property up to the council's standard. Your job is to get that work done and then book the surveyor back out.
- Social housing provider: A social housing provider is the organisation that sits between you and the council. You supply the property; they supply the tenants, the contract, the support and the guaranteed rent. Knowing who they are is the first step to finding them.
- Supported accommodation: One of Sean's two social housing strategies. You house homeless people who are unemployed and have a mental health need, and you provide specific support based on their needs, with the aim of moving them to independent living within 6 to 12 months. It is more complex than temporary accommodation, with more red tape.
- Temporary accommodation: Housing that a council uses for people and families who cannot get a permanent, stable home. Shaun calls it the buzz word in property right now and the strategy he is using to scale in 2025. You take part by supplying properties the council can place these people in.
- VA (virtual assistant): A VA is a virtual assistant, someone who works for you remotely on admin such as arranging appointments. You can hire from the UK or from overseas, for example the Philippines, Nigeria or South Africa, where rates are far lower. Shaun paid around $3 to $5 an hour and agrees it was a game changer.
- Volume sales vs prestige sales: Shaun splits car selling into two categories. Volume sales is what Ford was. Prestige sales is what BMW came under, selling cars with a higher value. He moved from the first to the second to make the job harder.
